6 Collective Communication

Purpose

Fleet stack focus for this chapter.

Why does communication between machines constrain large-scale machine learning systems?

Computation scales by adding processors; communication scales by moving data between them. These scale differently. Adding processors increases available arithmetic capacity, but useful work stalls when the required data does not arrive in time. Exchanging gradients, activations, and parameters can then consume more time than computing them. On one machine, shortening the dominant calculation often shortens the job. Across a fleet, that same improvement may simply make each processor reach its next dependency sooner and wait longer for the others. The cost of that wait includes every reserved accelerator, so a small delay repeated throughout training can consume a substantial budget without advancing the model. Faster links help when transfer capacity is the constraint, but the benefit depends on which physical paths the traffic shares. More bandwidth cannot remove a dependency. Sending less data introduces its own choices about numerical accuracy, memory use, and when the next computation can begin. An exchange must also deliver the correct result to every participant that needs it. A fast transfer that combines the wrong contributions does not preserve a valid training run. The engineering decision is therefore how to organize communication so that its cost remains compatible with the work it enables. Light-speed delays, bandwidth limits, and the energy cost of movement make that decision as physical as processor design. In C³ terms, collective communication connects distributed compute through coordinated exchanges whose correctness and exposed cost determine whether the fleet delivers useful scale.

Learning Objectives
  • Apply communication cost models to estimate collective duration and message-size crossover points
  • Match collective communication primitives and AllToAll schedules to parallelism strategies, topology, message granularity, and skew
  • Compare ring, tree, butterfly, double-tree, and hierarchical reduction algorithms using scale-dependent cost models
  • Evaluate topology-aware collective schedules under link-bandwidth, rank-placement, and shared-capacity constraints
  • Evaluate compression, sparsification, and error feedback by balancing communication savings against convergence risk
  • Design overlap strategies with bucket fusion, asynchronous operations, and layer-by-layer gradient scheduling
  • Specify matched measurements for calibrating collective cost models across algorithms, process groups, and topologies

6.1 From Parallelism to Communication Patterns

When 10,000 GPUs cooperate on one training update, the splits created by data, tensor, pipeline, and expert parallelism determine which results must cross device boundaries. Workers execute collective operations within participating groups and point-to-point transfers between pipeline stages. Within the multi-tier fleet stack diagrammed in The Fleet Stack, these exchanges occupy the Distributed Execution row of the Distribution band, where logical parallelism becomes physical traffic.

The 3D parallelism cube assumes that replicas, shards, stages, and experts can exchange gradients, activations, parameters, and tokens quickly enough for the optimizer to see one coherent training run. Distributed Training decided which exchanges each partition requires and charged them to the Fleet Law’s communication term \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) as budgets. That assumption is the handoff from parallelism to communication. Every way of splitting the model relocates work onto the network, and this chapter turns those budgets into schedules whose latency, bandwidth, and topology costs can be computed.

↩︎ The 3D parallelism cube, which composes data, pipeline, and tensor parallelism into one configuration space, is established in Single-Machine to Distributed Transition.

The first distinction is between an operation’s result and the schedule that produces it. The Synchronization Backbone named AllReduce, AllGather, and AllToAll as the traffic the fabric must carry; here they become contracts on results. AllReduce combines corresponding tensor elements across participating workers and returns the reduced tensor to every worker. AllGather instead concatenates their shards, ReduceScatter combines contributions but leaves each worker with only a shard of the result, and AllToAll sends different data to different destinations. The parallel decomposition determines which contract each exchange needs; ring, tree, and hierarchical algorithms are alternative schedules for carrying that contract out.

The gap between the cube’s assumption and the wire reveals an asymmetry in how computation and communication scale. Computation is local because each GPU works on its own data, so aggregate compute grows with the number of GPUs. Communication is global because keeping the fleet synchronized requires information to cross physical links with finite latency, bandwidth, interconnect topology, and energy. Gradient synchronization is the central case, because every synchronous data-parallel step repeats it on the full gradient. Scaling laws (principle 9) make that case harder over time, because the capability they reward comes from more parameters trained on more accelerators, so the gradient to be reduced and the group that must agree on it grow together.

For the initial gradient budget, consider a ring schedule: each worker passes tensor chunks to a neighbor, first accumulating their sums and then circulating the completed chunks. Each phase moves nearly one tensor’s worth of data per worker, so the bandwidth budget is approximately twice the gradient size divided by link bandwidth. The detailed round count follows once the transfer model is established.

Definition 6.1: Gradient synchronization

Gradient synchronization is the collective communication step in synchronous data-parallel training in which every worker contributes its locally computed gradient tensor to an aggregate reduction, then receives the same reduced result so all model replicas apply an identical update.

  1. Significance: Communicating all gradients of a 70B-parameter model in BF16 gives a 140 GB logical payload per replica per step. As a communication-only baseline, suppose every rank reduces that complete buffer. A ring across 1,000 GPUs at 50 GB/s per link has a bandwidth budget of approximately \(2 \times 140/50\) \(\approx\) 5.6 s of transfer per step. Model partitioning shrinks each rank’s share of that buffer, and only the portion of \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) left exposed after overlap adds to step time in the Fleet Law (equation 1.3).
  2. Distinction: Unlike a parameter server, which concentrates every worker’s gradient at a central aggregator, ring AllReduce spreads the exchange so that each worker sends \(2 \times (\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize\) bytes per step, approaching \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) however large the cluster grows (section 6.6.1 develops the comparison).
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that ring AllReduce behaves like an all-to-all exchange. In ring AllReduce, each worker communicates with neighbors according to a schedule, and the per-node communication volume is approximately constant as the cluster grows. The scaling pressure comes from latency steps, topology, and the gradient volume per step, not from every worker opening a distinct stream to every other worker.

For standard synchronous data-parallel stochastic gradient descent (SGD), gradient synchronization supplies the shared gradient needed for one optimization trajectory. Replicas that apply different local gradients can diverge. To reproduce the intended global-batch update, they must also start from matching parameters and optimizer state, weight local contributions correctly, and apply the same update rule. The AllReduce1 primitive provides the reduction; convergence follows from the optimization assumptions, not from communication alone. Floating-point reduction order can still change low-order bits.

1 AllReduce: The name comes from MPI and describes the result, not the schedule: a Reduce (combining every rank’s data at one root) followed by a Broadcast (returning the combined value to all ranks). A direct gather to the root concentrates raw input traffic there, but a tree can aggregate contributions along the way. The algorithms in section 6.6.3 distribute the reduction without a root.

2 Ring AllReduce: The algorithm dates to the HPC collective-communication literature; Patarasuk and Yuan analyzed bandwidth-optimal AllReduce algorithms for workstation clusters, Baidu’s Andrew Gibiansky popularized ring AllReduce for deep learning in February 2017, and Horovod plus NVIDIA NCCL helped make it a common distributed-training primitive (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009; Gibiansky 2017; Sergeev and Balso 2018; Jeaugey 2017).

Patarasuk, Pitch, and Xin Yuan. 2009. “Bandwidth Optimal All-Reduce Algorithms for Clusters of Workstations.” Journal of Parallel and Distributed Computing 69 (2): 117–24. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jpdc.2008.09.002.
Gibiansky, A. 2017. Bringing HPC Techniques to Deep Learning. Baidu Research Technical Blog.
Sergeev, Alexander, and Mike Del Balso. 2018. “Horovod: Fast and Easy Distributed Deep Learning in TensorFlow.” CoRR abs/1802.05799.

The gradient payload depends on parameter count and the representation transmitted. A model with \(\Parameters\) parameters contributes \(2\Parameters\) bytes per data-parallel replica when all gradients are communicated in BF16 (2 bytes per parameter); stored weight precision alone does not determine that choice. For a 70-billion-parameter model, a mid-sized dense model smaller than the 175B-parameter running instance of Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3), this gives 140 GB for the complete logical gradient. A ring moves nearly twice its input buffer size per rank.2 If a replica spans multiple ranks through model parallelism, each corresponding-shard group reduces its own portion rather than this full buffer.

This chapter varies the volume’s reference configuration in two stated ways. Its headline payload is this 70B gradient rather than the 175B instance’s, which keeps the worked arithmetic compact; every bandwidth term in the chapter is linear in payload, so each result scales directly to the larger model, and section 6.7.1 prices the 175B instance on the volume’s reference cluster itself. The cluster size changes from example to example, because each algorithm’s behavior depends on rank count and the point of an example is often to isolate one term. Each worked example states its rank count. A second recurring payload, the chapter’s reference gradient buffer, first appears in the energy estimate of section 6.2.

At large scale (hundreds of billions of parameters across thousands of GPUs), gradient synchronization can dominate training step time unless collective communication is systematically optimized. Understanding how to manage that overhead requires modeling data movement as physics rather than as an abstract API call.

Exposed gradient synchronization can dominate step time at scale.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A dense-model training run triples its GPU count from 64 to 192 while holding the global work per update fixed. Step time drops by only 30%, and per-GPU compute utilization falls. Which scaling mechanism should the team investigate, without treating these aggregates as proof of the cause?

    1. Backpropagation stops functioning correctly across multiple nodes because gradients cannot be computed concurrently across independent data batches.
    2. Floating-point accumulation during gradient reduction becomes numerically unstable when more than 64 GPUs participate in the collective.
    3. Host CPU memory bandwidth saturates because all optimizer states must be shuffled through host DRAM on every multi-node step.
    4. Local compute can shrink while global synchronization and shared-link transfers remain exposed; rank-aligned traces are needed to determine whether communication actually limits the step.

  2. A machine learning engineer evaluates the communication traffic induced by different model parallelism strategies. According to the chapter travel manifest, which mapping correctly pairs a parallelism strategy with its primary collective primitive and primary physical constraint?

    1. Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP) -> Broadcast primitive -> Memory capacity constraint.
    2. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) Routing -> AllToAll primitive -> Latency and bisection network contention constraint.
    3. Tensor Parallelism within a node -> Point-to-Point Send/Recv -> Inter-node bisection bandwidth constraint.
    4. Data Parallelism -> Reduce primitive -> Host-to-device PCIe bandwidth constraint.

  3. A logical Ring AllReduce cost model uses a 140 GB input per rank, corresponding to the model-wide BF16 gradient size of a 70B-parameter model. Holding that input fixed, a team expects per-rank sent volume to grow over 100 times when the group grows from 8 to 1,000 ranks. Explain why the expectation is false and why time can still increase. Treat this as transfer arithmetic, not a claim that a full replicated training state fits on one GPU.

  4. True or False: In synchronous data-parallel distributed training, using Ring AllReduce causes each GPU network bandwidth requirement to scale quadratically with the number of GPUs \(N\) because every worker must open a direct communication socket to every other worker in the cluster.

  5. On a supported hardware and transport path, the mechanism that lets a NIC read and write GPU memory without staging payloads through host DRAM is known as ____ RDMA.

See Answers →

6.2 The Physics of Data Movement

Physical constraints govern data movement before algorithms are designed. The speed of light sets a latency floor3 (roughly 5 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) per kilometer in fiber), the bandwidth-distance product limits how far a fast link can reach before it needs optics (signal loss grows with both lane rate and cable length, so copper reach shrinks as lane rates rise; Reach and medium for copper and optics), and kernel-bypass transports such as remote direct memory access (RDMA) and GPUDirect RDMA strip the per-message software tax down to a few microseconds. Collective communication adds the energy cost of moving a bit, which the algorithms must respect as firmly as latency and bandwidth.

3 The Speed of Light Constraint: Light travels through optical fiber at approximately 200,000 km/s, or 200 meters per microsecond. For a rack-to-rack fiber path of 100 meters, propagation alone contributes approximately 500 ns in one direction. Faster switches can reduce other latency components, but reducing this propagation delay requires a shorter path or a different propagation medium.

↩︎ Kernel-bypass RDMA, which strips the per-message software overhead, and GPUDirect RDMA, which removes the host-memory bounce, are established in RDMA and GPUDirect.

Moving data consumes energy across storage arrays, interfaces, serializers, transceivers, and switches. A transfer that starts in local SRAM can stay on-package in high-bandwidth memory (HBM), cross to an intra-node peer GPU, or traverse intermediate switches to a remote node, and each step widens the physical accounting boundary that an energy estimate must include (figure 1). Each boundary activates distinct physical components: on-package links, intra-node crossbars, serializers, optical transceivers, and switch fabrics. Distance alone does not determine energy per bit, and an endpoint-only estimate cannot be compared directly with a complete fabric-path estimate. Locality remains useful because it can avoid transfers and active interfaces; the energy saving must be evaluated under the same workload, utilization, and accounting boundary.

The chapter’s reference gradient buffer of 1 GB stands for one large fused reduction, far smaller than a full model gradient but large enough that per-byte transfer, not per-message startup, governs its cost; later examples return to it by name. Reducing it around an illustrative 10,000-rank ring sends approximately twice the payload from every rank. At a complete-path transfer cost of 35 pJ/bit, a representative midpoint of the range typical of switched fabrics rather than a measurement of any particular one, this gives about 5.6 kJ per reduction, paid again at every synchronous step. The usable rule is that communication energy is the bytes an algorithm sends multiplied by the energy per bit of every boundary those bytes cross. An algorithm that moves fewer bytes, or a placement that keeps more of them inside the node, therefore saves energy as directly as it saves time, while the share of the step’s total energy that communication represents must be measured at the same accounting boundary as the compute it accompanies.

These constraints enter different parts of a communication budget. Latency sets the floor for every message regardless of size. Bandwidth caps the throughput for large transfers. Protocol overhead adds a per-message tax that penalizes fine-grained communication, which is why the kernel-bypass transports matter for collective performance. RDMA’s zero-copy path4 avoids staging each transfer through host memory and the kernel’s socket buffers. The physical fabric supplies these limits; the collective schedule determines how many times the job pays them.

4 Zero-Copy Communication: GPUDirect RDMA lets the NIC read gradients directly from accelerator memory, so a 140 GB gradient never passes through host DRAM, whereas a staged path writes and then rereads it there (280 GB of host-memory traffic per send).

The initial ring budget reveals why data movement can become the governing constraint at scale. A single AllReduce on a 70B model’s gradients consumes seconds of wall-clock time, which becomes exposed delay when no independent computation overlaps it. This asymmetry between local computation and global coordination motivates every collective algorithm that follows, and it is why a seemingly modest improvement to the collective repays itself across a training campaign that repeats it at every step.

Across the four scale tiers charted in figure 2, the training step’s critical path inverts as a fixed workload spreads from 8 to 4,096 GPUs. In this illustrative scenario, local compute contracts from 75 percent on a single 8-GPU node down to 20 percent at 4,096 GPUs, while communication and synchronization expand to claim 80 percent (65 percent communication and 15 percent sync/idle) of elapsed wall-clock time. These fractions represent assumed scaling regimes rather than empirical measurements for a specific cluster; in practice, gradient size, local batch size, partition topology, and asynchronous overlap determine where the critical path lies.

6.3 The Gradient’s Travel Manifest

Data movement begins as gradients become ready during the backward pass. A synchronous data-parallel worker computes gradients from its local batch, and the optimizer requires the combined global contributions before applying the parameter update. Some exchanges can begin while independent backward computation continues. Tensor ownership determines the destination; gradient readiness determines when transfers can start.

The travel manifest for this exchange is dictated by the chosen parallelism strategy, because the mathematical decomposition determines which tensors must cross device boundaries. For Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3), dense gradient contributions are combined within each data-parallel group; in a hybrid layout, that group contains the matching parameter shard from each replica. AllReduce serves the replicated-gradient baseline. For Archetype B (DLRM at Scale),5 lookup requests travel to embedding-shard owners and the resulting vectors return to the workers that need them. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) routing similarly sends activations to the ranks hosting selected experts and returns their outputs. These destination-specific exchanges motivate AllToAll.

5 DLRM (Deep Learning Recommendation Model): Meta’s 2019 architecture for click-through rate prediction uses sharded embedding tables and exchanges the resulting sparse lookups across workers. The resulting AllToAll may be startup-bound when each destination receives a fine-grained message, injection- or reception-bound as aggregate payload grows, or fabric-bound under oversubscription, unfavorable routing, or shard-access skew. DLRM therefore supplies a destination-specific traffic pattern, not a universal latency regime.

↩︎ The three lighthouse archetypes and the C³ regimes each one stresses are established in Three systems archetypes.

At large scale, these parallelism strategies can be combined. A 3D parallelism layout combines data parallelism, tensor parallelism, and pipeline parallelism, creating distinct communication groups whose exchanges may overlap in time. In a node-local tensor-parallel example, layer reductions use NVLink, pipeline stages exchange activations and their gradients point-to-point across nodes, and data-parallel groups reduce matching parameter shards over InfiniBand. Other placements change those physical paths while retaining the logical communication requirements.

Each primitive operates on a different process group, a subset of the total GPU population that participates in that particular collective. Classical Message Passing Interface (MPI) uses a communicator as the object that names such a participating set; its communicator size is the number of ranks in that set. MPI libraries already selected collective algorithms based on communicator size and message size (Thakur et al. 2005). GPU communication libraries inherit that algorithm-selection problem and add the further challenge of coordinating overlapping process groups without creating contention between concurrent collectives.

Table 1 maps each parallelism strategy to the data it must move, the collective primitive that moves it, and the term most likely to bind. The table acts as a traffic map that specifies which participants must exchange data. The network transfer model then prices those exchanges, and the collective algorithms that follow provide execution schedules.

In this table, expert parallelism refers to the mixture-of-experts6 architecture pattern. The mapping shows that different parallelism strategies impose fundamentally different communication patterns. Data parallelism and fully sharded data parallel (FSDP) commonly generate large messages that benefit from ring-based algorithms and hierarchical decomposition. Tensor and pipeline parallelism generate smaller, more frequent messages whose cost sits on the critical path, so low-overhead software stacks and fast local links matter. Expert parallelism and sharded embedding tables generate destination-specific traffic whose limiting term depends on message granularity, endpoint injection and reception, fabric bisection, oversubscription, routing, and skew.

6 Mixture-of-Experts (MoE): Because each token activates only a few experts, MoE replaces a uniform dense reduction with destination-specific AllToAll traffic. Fine-grained dispatch can expose startup cost, larger batches can bind injection or reception bandwidth, and oversubscription, routing, or expert skew can make bisection capacity or a hotspot the critical path.

The manifest’s rows describe data center parallelism, so they leave out the federated Archetype C. Across all three lighthouse archetypes, the dominant exchange and its bottleneck differ.

Lighthouse 6.1: Communication archetype patterns
The travel manifest for a gradient depends on the system’s objective function and constraint regime. Each lighthouse archetype faces a distinct communication challenge, and the techniques developed in this chapter map to those challenges differently, as table 2 summarizes.

The table separates traffic pattern from limiting resource. A dense reduction, a destination-specific exchange, and an intermittent device update require different interventions even though all three serve a training workload. Hierarchical AllReduce, AllToAll schedule selection, and compression with error feedback each receive their own treatment later in the chapter.

Choosing among those interventions requires pricing each exchange, and pricing requires a model of network performance.

6.4 Network Performance Modeling

Predicting transfer duration across a cluster requires separating two physical variables: the time required to initiate a message and the rate at which data moves once in transit. Across a data center fabric, these two terms govern communication latency and throughput differently depending on message size, network topology, and software overhead.

6.4.1 The alpha-beta cost model for network transfers

The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model \(\PointToPointTransferTime(\PointToPointMessageSize) = \MessageStartupLatency+ \PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\) prices a transfer over a single link (principle 5), separating a fixed startup tax (\(\MessageStartupLatency\)) from a per-byte transit fee (\(\PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\)) (Hockney 1994). A collective pays that price at every step of its schedule, so the two terms, multiplied by the schedule’s round count and traffic factor, determine algorithm selection across the fleet.

Hockney, Roger W. 1994. “The Communication Challenge for MPP: Intel Paragon and Meiko CS-2.” Parallel Computing 20 (3): 389–98. https://doi.org/10.1016/s0167-8191(06)80021-9.

↩︎ The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model for a single link and its latency-dominated and bandwidth-dominated regimes are established in The \MessageStartupLatency-\LinkBandwidth performance model.

↪ Worked message-size regimes and crossover calculations for this model are developed in The \MessageStartupLatency-\LinkBandwidth Communication Model.

For collectives, the parameters must describe the effective path. Latency (\(\MessageStartupLatency\)) is the startup cost paid at every step of a schedule, so it includes recurring software overhead, PCIe traversal, and network switching time; one-time communicator initialization is a separate setup cost. Bandwidth (\(\LinkBandwidth\)) is the sustained per-direction transfer rate. The critical message size \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize = \MessageStartupLatency\cdot \LinkBandwidth\), where the two terms are equal, carries over unchanged, but a collective changes what must be compared with it. Each step of a schedule transfers a chunk rather than the whole tensor, and a ring over \(\DeviceCount\) ranks moves \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) bytes per step. A bucket of tens of megabytes split across a thousand ranks therefore yields chunks of tens of kilobytes, below the inter-node crossovers in table 3, even though the bucket itself is far above them. Below \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), a step is latency-bound; above it, bandwidth-bound.

The crossover explains two recurring optimizations. For InfiniBand NDR at \(\MessageStartupLatency\approx\) 1.5 μs and \(\LinkBandwidth\approx\) 50 GB/s, the knee sits at \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize \approx\) 75 KB, the value The \MessageStartupLatency-\LinkBandwidth performance model computed for a single transfer. A 4 KB routing message is far below \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), so the startup tax dominates. If 100 ready messages have compatible destinations and can share one transfer, fusing them into a 400 KB message reduces the modeled cost from approximately \(100\MessageStartupLatency=\) 150 μs to \(\MessageStartupLatency+ \PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\approx\) 9.5 μs, roughly a 16× improvement before packing costs. More generally, \(\SplitMessageCount\) serialized messages of size \(\PointToPointMessageSize/\SplitMessageCount\) can cost up to \(\SplitMessageCount\times\) as much as one message of size \(\PointToPointMessageSize\) when \(\PointToPointMessageSize/\SplitMessageCount\ll \LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\). Fusion is the first optimization, and it motivates framework gradient buckets and destination-aware token coalescing.

Compression is the second, and the crossover tells where it pays. A 140 GB gradient tensor sits far above \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), so shrinking its bytes shrinks the dominant \(\PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\) term directly, provided encoding and aggregation costs are counted. Even keeping only the largest 1 percent of values (Top-k sparsification, developed in section 6.9) leaves 1.4 GB of values before their indices are added, still far above \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), so the saving remains a byte saving. Compressing a 4 KB routing message, by contrast, offers little, because its unchanged \(\MessageStartupLatency\) term already dominates.

Across the typical data center interconnects tabulated in table 3, the critical-size column carries the load-bearing pattern: intra-node NVLink stays latency-bound up to several hundred kilobytes, whereas the inter-node fabrics cross over below roughly 100 KB. A message between those crossover sizes can therefore be latency-bound inside a node but bandwidth-bound once it travels between nodes.

The same comparison across generations shows that the roughly ninefold per-direction cliff between a GPU’s NVLink capacity and its one InfiniBand port, which Bandwidth hierarchy established for the H100 generation, is not an artifact of one product cycle (figure 3). Every point in the figure is per direction, so NVLink values are half the bidirectional datasheet totals, exactly as for the NVLink 4.0 row of table 3. A gap of that order has persisted for a decade even as both tiers grew. A GPU’s NVLink aggregate and a single network port are different endpoint boundaries, but that persistent gap is why collective design must be hierarchy-aware, because unpartitioned traffic routed across the slower external fabric saturates it long before the local links are busy.

The opening of this section claimed that a bucket far above \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\) can still be carried in chunks below it, because a ring divides the bucket among its \(\DeviceCount\) ranks. Pricing one bucket at two group sizes tests that claim and shows what it costs.

Napkin Math 6.1: The critical message size
Problem: A data-parallel job reduces a 25 MB gradient bucket with Ring AllReduce over InfiniBand NDR, with \(\MessageStartupLatency=\) 1.5 μs and \(\LinkBandwidth=\) 50 GB/s. The bucket is hundreds of times larger than the single-transfer knee. Does each step of the ring run in the bandwidth-bound regime on 64 GPUs, and on the 1,024-GPU reference cluster?

Math:

  1. Knee: \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize = \MessageStartupLatency\cdot \LinkBandwidth=\) \(1.5 \times 10^{-6}\) s \(\times\) \(5 \times 10^{10}\) B/s \(=\) 75 KB.
  2. Chunk per step: A ring sends \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) per step, 390.6 KB on 64 ranks (above the knee) but only 24.4 KB on 1,024 ranks (below it).
  3. Ring time \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\left(\MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\DeviceCount\LinkBandwidth}\right)\): about 1.2 ms on 64 ranks, of which the byte term is 1.0 ms; about 4.1 ms on 1,024 ranks, where the byte term is still only 1.0 ms.

Systems insight: The regime belongs to the chunk, not to the tensor. The byte term barely moves as the group grows, but at 1,024 ranks each step carries a chunk below \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), so startup dominates and the same bucket takes several times longer. Large language model (LLM) gradients are bandwidth-bound as tensors, yet a flat ring over a large group can still carry them in latency-bound steps. The levers then shift to larger buckets, fewer rounds, and hierarchy, which is why the algorithms of section 6.6 compete on round count as well as bytes.

Below \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\) the latency term \(\MessageStartupLatency\) dominates; above it, the byte term \(\PointToPointMessageSize/\LinkBandwidth\) does.

For collectives, the two regimes map onto different levers. In the latency-bound regime \((\PointToPointMessageSize< \LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize)\), where MoE routing tokens, scalar reductions, and the small chunks of a large-group schedule sit, the levers are fusion, fewer schedule rounds, fewer hops, and a leaner software stack. In the bandwidth-bound regime \((\PointToPointMessageSize\gg \LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize)\), where LLM gradients and optimizer states sit, they are compression, a byte-efficient algorithm, and link aggregation across multiple network interface cards (NICs). Classifying each exchange, and each chunk within it, into one regime is the diagnostic skill the rest of the chapter applies.

Checkpoint 6.1: Alpha-beta diagnostics

Verify your understanding of network performance regimes:

6.4.2 The LogP model

The α-β model estimates transfer duration without specifying how much independent computation can proceed during that transfer. Adding the entire estimate to compute time therefore overstates step time when overlap is possible. The LogP model (Culler et al. 1993) separates network transit from processor involvement with four parameters:

Culler, David, Richard Karp, David Patterson, Abhijit Sahay, Klaus Erik Schauser, Eunice Santos, Ramesh Subramonian, and Thorsten von Eicken. 1993. “LogP: Towards a Realistic Model of Parallel Computation.” ACM SIGPLAN Notices 28 (7): 1–12. https://doi.org/10.1145/173284.155333.
  • \(\LogpNetworkLatency\) (latency): The time for a message to traverse the network (similar to \(\MessageStartupLatency\)).
  • \(\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) (overhead): The processor time spent initiating or completing a transfer. During this work, the same execution resource cannot compute, making \(\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) the nonoverlappable cost. In a distributed training context, dispatch, device-kernel launch, memory registration, and runtime progress can all contribute. The value is an effective parameter of a particular software and hardware path, so it must be measured with the same collective, process group, and topology used by the workload.
  • \(\MessageInjectionGap\) (gap): The minimum spacing between consecutive sends or consecutive receives at a processor. It limits endpoint message rate but does not describe every source of link contention in a routed fabric.
  • \(\DeviceCount\) (rank count): The number of ranks in the communication group. The original model names this processor count with its final letter, P; this chapter writes it \(\DeviceCount\) to match its other collective cost formulas.

LogP distinguishes network latency (\(\LogpNetworkLatency\), which can be hidden) from processor overhead (\(\SymmetricMessageOverhead\), which cannot). Independent computation can overlap the network transit interval, while initiation and completion overhead remain exposed on the execution resource that performs them. A small overlap calculation makes this distinction concrete:

Napkin Math 6.2: Hiding communication behind computation
Problem: A training pipeline attempts to overlap gradient AllReduce with the next layer’s backward pass. The backward pass takes 500 μs. Represent the complete transfer by a toy budget with network time 100 μs, processor overhead \(\SymmetricMessageOverhead=\) 50 μs to initiate, and \(\SymmetricMessageOverhead=\) 50 μs to receive. Assume both overhead intervals occupy the same execution resource as backward computation, with independent network progress and no contention. Can the communication be hidden under these assumptions?

Math:

  1. Overlappable portion: Network latency \(\LogpNetworkLatency\) = 100 μs (data in flight while GPU computes).
  2. Non-overlappable portion: \(2\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) = 100 μs (GPU busy initiating/receiving).
  3. Compute available: 500 μs.
  4. Hidden: All 100 μs of \(\LogpNetworkLatency\) can overlap with compute.
  5. Exposed: The 100 μs of \(2\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) overhead cannot overlap.

Result: Effective time is 100 μs + max(500 μs, 100 μs) = 600 μs. The network latency is hidden, but the processor overhead remains exposed. Applied to every layer of a backward pass, the same split determines which gradient transfers a training step can hide (section 6.10.1).

Systems insight: The α-β model captures the total communication time. The LogP model reveals how much of it can be hidden. When designing pipelined training, optimize for low \(\SymmetricMessageOverhead\) (kernel bypass, GPUDirect) rather than high \(\LinkBandwidth\) alone.

The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model estimates transfer duration from latency and bandwidth measurements. LogP adds the processor/network distinction needed to estimate exposed delay in pipelined execution. Choosing a communication schedule therefore requires both a transfer budget and the independent computation available to overlap it.

When messages are large and pipelined across the network, the LogGP model (Alexandrov et al. 1995) extends LogP with a gap-per-byte parameter \(\LongMessageGapPerByte\), modeling the streaming cost of long messages. These models describe multiprocessor communication with endpoint rate and capacity limits; they are not restricted to one flow on one link. Their compact parameters do not explicitly represent every shared path, oversubscribed cut, or routing hotspot. For congestion-sensitive workloads, particularly AllToAll for MoE, measurements on the target placement must test whether the model captures the active bottleneck.

Alexandrov, Albert D., Mihai F. Ionescu, Klaus E. Schauser, and Chris J. Scheiman. 1995. “LogGP: Incorporating Long Messages into the LogP Model—One Step Closer Towards a Realistic Model for Parallel Computation.” Proceedings of the Seventh Annual ACM Symposium on Parallel Algorithms and Architectures - SPAA ’95 30: 95–105. https://doi.org/10.1145/215399.215427.

↪ The LogGP long-message cost model and its gap parameters are developed in The LogGP model extension.

The transfer model prices one message, but a training dependency usually requires a coordinated set of messages. Counting algorithmic rounds or bytes first requires a precise statement of which result each participating worker must receive.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A single-transfer model uses effective startup cost \(\alpha=1.5\,\mu\text{s}\) and per-direction bandwidth \(\beta=50\text{ GB/s}\). Which conclusion follows for a 4 KB transfer, and what must be added before classifying a complete gradient AllReduce?

    1. The single-transfer knee is 75 KB, so startup dominates the 4 KB transfer. A complete AllReduce additionally requires the schedule’s round count and chunk size, then calibration for its placement.
    2. The 4 KB transfer is bandwidth-bound because adding ranks makes every individual transfer saturate its port.
    3. The 75 KB knee is a maximum buffer size, so larger messages cannot be sent over this link.
    4. The critical message size defines the maximum possible buffer size that can be transferred in a single CUDA stream without host CPU intervention.

  2. In a toy overlap budget, independent computation takes \(600\,\mu\text{s}\) and the complete network interval takes \(200\,\mu\text{s}\). Initiation and completion each cost \(50\,\mu\text{s}\) on the same execution resource as that computation. Assume independent network progress and no contention. What is the effective elapsed time, and which component remains exposed?

    1. Effective time is \(600\,\mu\text{s}\), and communication is 100% hidden because \(L_{\text{lat}} < T_{\text{compute}}\).
    2. Effective time is \(800\,\mu\text{s}\), because network latency and processor overhead must both be added directly to compute time.
    3. Effective time is \(750\,\mu\text{s}\), because the gap parameter \(g\) adds an irreducible stall to the compute pipeline.
    4. Effective time is \(700\,\mu\text{s}\) (\(2o + \max(T_{\text{compute}}, L_{\text{lat}})\)), where the \(200\,\mu\text{s}\) network latency is completely hidden behind the \(600\,\mu\text{s}\) compute, but the \(100\,\mu\text{s}\) of processor overhead (\(2o\)) remains exposed.

  3. Explain why small-message collective timings can exceed a bare-wire prediction by a much larger relative factor than large-message timings. Which measurements are needed before using this difference in a cluster capacity plan?

  4. True or False: In the stated toy LogP budget with independent work, network progress, and no contention, upgrading a network fabric to achieve lower physical wire latency (\(L_{\text{lat}}\)) will not reduce step time if the overlapped computation window \(T_{\text{compute}}\) already exceeds \(L_{\text{lat}}\) and the nonoverlappable processor overhead \(o\) remains unchanged.

  5. Sequence the hierarchy of communication cost models and diagnostic analyses in order from the simplest zero-overlap first-order sizing to real-world cluster validation: (1) empirically benchmark bare collectives with nccl-tests to capture cluster-specific reality gaps and contention, (2) compute critical message size \(n^* = \alpha \cdot \beta\) using the \(\alpha\)-\(\beta\) model to classify latency vs bandwidth regimes, (3) profile processor overhead \(o\) and overlappable latency \(L_{\text{lat}}\) using the LogP model to assess overlap feasibility with the backward pass.

See Answers →

6.5 Collective Operation Primitives

Sending a gradient tensor to a peer does not specify which workers must contribute or where the final result belongs. Collective operations supply that group-level contract, using a vocabulary standardized by MPI and inherited by modern ML communication libraries (Message Passing Interface Forum 2015). The four contracts sketched at the start of the chapter differ in what each rank holds afterward (figure 4). AllReduce leaves an identical sum (\(\Glyph{\Sigma}\)) on every rank, AllGather leaves every rank with the concatenation of all shards, ReduceScatter sums across ranks but leaves each rank one reduced shard (\(\Glyph{\Sigma}_i\)), and AllToAll performs a distributed matrix transpose, routing each rank’s \(\DestinationRankIndex\)-th chunk to rank \(\DestinationRankIndex\). Two further primitives complete the set and serve as building blocks for these four: Broadcast, in which rank 0 sends its data to all ranks, and Reduce, in which all ranks aggregate into rank 0.

↪ The bandwidth and latency cost terms of AllReduce, AllGather, ReduceScatter, AllToAll, and Broadcast, under ring, tree, and pairwise-exchange algorithms, are developed in Collective Operation Complexity.

Definition 6.2: Collective operation

Collective operation is a distributed communication pattern in which every rank in a process group invokes a matching operation to aggregate, broadcast, or redistribute data. The operation’s contract specifies the output at each rank; it does not require simultaneous entry and does not, by itself, impose a general-purpose barrier before or after the call (Message Passing Interface Forum 2015).

  1. Significance: Because the contract names only each rank’s result, the schedule that carries it decides how communication demand spreads as the group grows. A schedule that funnels every contribution through one endpoint makes the communication term \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) of the Fleet Law (equation 1.3) grow with \(\DeviceCount\), while a balanced schedule keeps each rank’s bytes nearly constant; section 6.6.1 quantifies the gap.
  2. Distinction: Unlike point-to-point communication, a collective defines one operation over a process group. Ranks may enter at different times, and some algorithms allow one rank to complete before another has entered; correctness depends on matching the operation sequence and argument contract across ranks, not on barrier semantics.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating a non-blocking collective as complete when its initiation call returns. The returned request or stream event represents work in flight. A dependent consumer must wait for completion, and a late rank can extend that dependency even though the collective is not a barrier.

The primitive choice is the first scaling decision because it fixes which ranks must coordinate and which result each must obtain. The algorithm and placement then determine the traffic and its limiting resource. Different model architectures require different operations, and choosing a broader operation than the workload needs can create unnecessary bottlenecks.

6.5.1 The six core primitives

With the contracts fixed, table 4 adds what the figure cannot show, the typical use and the cost of each primitive. Its rows run from the root-centered patterns (Broadcast and Reduce), through the three in which every rank contributes to and receives part or all of one shared tensor (AllReduce, AllGather, and ReduceScatter), to AllToAll, the only one in which every pair of ranks exchanges its own data. That order describes routing, not cost. ReduceScatter and AllGather each move half of AllReduce’s bytes, because each is one phase of it. The selection rule is to choose the contract whose output matches what the next computation consumes, since a broader result than the model needs (a full AllReduce where the optimizer only reads a shard, for example) moves bytes that no rank uses. The cost column previews the algorithms derived in section 6.6, using \(\DeviceCount\) for the participating rank count, \(\CollectiveTensorSize\) for the full tensor, and \(\CollectivePayloadSize\) for the per-rank AllToAll input payload. For AllReduce, the full tensor has the same byte size as each rank’s input payload, the convention used in the following algorithm models. Two schedules appear there. A ring passes chunks between neighbors, and the simple full-message tree reduces up a binary tree and broadcasts back down it, forwarding the whole tensor at each of its \(2\log_2 \DeviceCount\) levels. For Broadcast and Reduce, the table counts startup rounds and bytes per tree edge separately; each traverses one tree depth, so the elapsed transfer cost accumulates across its levels. The Tree AllReduce derivation combines those two traversals.

Ring achieves AllReduce’s bandwidth-optimal cost while the simple full-message Tree model trades bandwidth for logarithmic latency (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009; Thakur et al. 2005).

7 FSDP (Fully Sharded Data Parallel): PyTorch’s fully sharded implementation follows the ZeRO-3 idea of sharding parameters, gradients, and optimizer states across \(\DeviceCount\) accelerators (Rajbhandari et al. 2020). The trade-off is communication frequency, because full sharding adds layer-level AllGather and ReduceScatter operations instead of relying only on data parallelism’s step-level AllReduce, making it more sensitive to the \(\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) overhead.

Rajbhandari, Samyam, Jeff Rasley, Olatunji Ruwase, and Yuxiong He. 2020. “ZeRO: Memory Optimizations Toward Training Trillion Parameter Models.” SC20: International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage and Analysis, 1–16. https://doi.org/10.1109/sc41405.2020.00024.

AllReduce decomposes into ReduceScatter followed by AllGather. The decomposition is operational as well as mathematical, since Ring AllReduce’s first phase is a ReduceScatter and its second phase is an AllGather. ReduceScatter performs arithmetic, so it does not invert AllGather despite reversing the direction of shard expansion. Full ZeRO-style sharding and PyTorch FSDP7 use related partitioning strategies: AllGather parameter shards before a module needs them and ReduceScatter gradients after backward so each rank retains only its shard for the optimizer step (Rajbhandari et al. 2020). Sharding parameters, gradients, and optimizer state across workers reduces persistent model-state memory roughly by \(\DeviceCount\times\), while temporary full-parameter buffers and prefetching determine peak memory.

The same AllGather/ReduceScatter pair also appears in sequence parallelism, where an AllGather reconstructs the activation view needed for a local operation and a later ReduceScatter redistributes the result along the sequence dimension. A primitive’s semantics stay fixed even when the tensor dimension being sharded changes, so every schedule developed for the pair applies to both uses.

Beyond defining data-movement dependencies, collective operations carry explicit interface and numerical contracts. At the interface level, every rank in a process group must issue collectives in a compatible sequence; mismatched operation orders can deadlock or pair unrelated buffers. Operations also enforce shape and type contracts. AllReduce requires matching element counts and datatypes across all ranks, whereas variable-size exchanges require explicit per-destination counts and displacements. These invariants form the contract between the parallel decomposition and the communication runtime: the model decides which tensor slices each rank owns, and the collective contract guarantees that those slices can be recombined deterministically.

Reduction order introduces a second, numerical contract. Floating-point addition is not associative, so ring, tree, and switch-assisted reductions may accumulate the same values in different orders and produce different low-order bits (Goldberg 1991). A correct AllReduce therefore guarantees the specified reduction over all contributions, not bitwise identity across algorithms, rank counts, or hardware. Reproducibility-sensitive workloads must fix the algorithm and process-group configuration or use a reproducible accumulation scheme; ordinary training should treat small order-dependent differences as numerical behavior while still checking for larger rank disagreement.

Goldberg, David. 1991. “What Every Computer Scientist Should Know about Floating-Point Arithmetic.” ACM Computing Surveys 23 (1): 5–48. https://doi.org/10.1145/103162.103163.

The contrast between AllToAll and AllReduce highlights a fundamental difference in how collective operations scale with cluster size.

Systems Perspective 6.1: AllToAll vs. AllReduce: Why scale differs
Ring AllReduce can pipeline a uniform payload through neighbor exchanges. AllToAll presents a different scaling problem because every rank has destination-specific traffic, but the logical peer count alone does not determine its cost.

An AllToAll over \(\DeviceCount\) ranks creates \(\DeviceCount(\DeviceCount-1)\) logical source-destination transfers, yet each rank’s aggregate payload can remain fixed under weak scaling. Its lower bound is governed by the largest of four terms: source injection time, destination reception time, traffic crossing the fabric bisection, and per-message startup or scheduling overhead. Oversubscription and routing determine how much physical capacity those flows share. Message granularity determines how often they pay \(\MessageStartupLatency\). Routing skew determines whether one destination or link becomes the critical path. A full-bisection network carrying balanced, coarse-grained traffic can therefore scale well despite the quadratic logical connection count, whereas an oversubscribed fabric or skewed expert assignment can stall at much smaller \(\DeviceCount\).

Expert parallelism and large-scale recommendation systems can reach a communication wall earlier than standard data parallelism when their traffic exhausts injection, reception, or bisection capacity, or when routing and destination skew create hotspots. The primitive determines the traffic matrix; the physical fabric and routing distribution determine the scaling ceiling.

Logical pairs grow quadratically while per-rank payload can stay flat.

To make the AllToAll pattern concrete, consider a mixture-of-experts model with 64 experts distributed across 8 GPUs (8 experts per GPU). During each forward pass, a gating network assigns each input token to one or more experts. The tokens assigned to experts on remote GPUs must be physically moved to those GPUs before computation can proceed, and the results must be moved back afterward.

Napkin Math 6.3: AllToAll for MoE token routing
Problem: An MoE model processes a batch of 4096 tokens across 8 GPUs (512 tokens per GPU). Each token is a 2048-dimensional hidden state in BF16 (4.096 KB per token). The gating network assigns each token to exactly 1 of 64 experts (8 experts per GPU). Assuming uniform routing (each expert receives 64 tokens), how much data does each GPU send and receive?

Math:

Each GPU holds 512 tokens that need to reach 64 different experts across 8 GPUs. With uniform routing, each GPU sends 64 tokens to each of the other 7 GPUs (keeping 64 tokens local).

  • Data per GPU-to-GPU transfer: 64 tokens \(\times\) 4.096 KB/token = 262.144 KB
  • Total data sent per GPU: 7 \(\times\) 262.144 KB = 1.84 MB
  • Total data received per GPU: 1.84 MB (symmetric)

Latency Analysis (InfiniBand NDR, \(\MessageStartupLatency=\) 1.5 μs, \(\LinkBandwidth=\) 50 GB/s):

Each of the 7 transfers is a 262.144 KB message. From the \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) model: \(\TransferTimeForPayload\) = 1.5 μs + 5 μs = 6.5 μs per transfer.

If serialized: 7 \(\times\) 6.5 μs = 45.5 μs. If all 7 transfers are issued concurrently, they still share the GPU’s 50 GB/s injection port. The aggregate injection lower bound is therefore \(\MessageStartupLatency+ \text{total bytes sent}/\LinkBandwidth=\) 38.2 μs, before contention and additional collective overhead. Full duplex permits simultaneous sending and receiving; it does not provide one full-bandwidth outgoing port per peer.

Systems insight: The injection result is only a lower bound. If the topology’s usable bisection bandwidth is \(\BisectionBandwidth\) and \(\CutCrossingDataVolume\) bytes must cross its minimum cut, the exchange also obeys \(\AllToAllExchangeTime\geq \CutCrossingDataVolume/\BisectionBandwidth\). Oversubscription reduces \(\BisectionBandwidth\), fine-grained peer messages add startup cost, and expert skew raises the maximum receive volume above the uniform estimate. The forward pass runs the AllToAll twice per layer, once for token dispatch and once for result collection; training also communicates gradients during backward.

With the six contracts defined, every row of the travel manifest in table 1 names a primitive whose output and traffic pattern are precise. The sharded data-parallel row repays closer study, because sharding changes how often those primitives run rather than which ones run.

6.5.2 FSDP communication patterns

Fully sharded data parallelism, developed in Distributed Training as a memory technique (Rajbhandari et al. 2020), keeps data parallelism’s mathematics but changes how its communication is invoked. A fully sharded rank holds only \(1/\DeviceCount\) of the parameters, so it calls AllGather before each layer’s forward pass to reconstruct that layer and ReduceScatter after the backward pass to leave each rank the gradients for its own shard, then frees the temporary full parameters.

Distributed Training counted the consequence, a displacement of overhead from memory to communication frequency. With one communication group per layer, a model with \(\LayerCount\) layers issues \(2\LayerCount\) collective calls per step where unbucketed data parallelism issues one AllReduce, or about \(3\LayerCount\) when parameters are resharded after forward and gathered again for backward. The byte volume changes far less. When parameters and gradients travel at the same width and the group is large, each rank sends roughly the AllReduce’s \(2\CollectiveTensorSize\), or about \(3\CollectiveTensorSize\) (\(1.5\times\)) with resharding, where \(\CollectiveTensorSize\) is the model-sized payload without optimizer state; different parameter and reduction datatypes shift that ratio. The call count carries its own price, because every call pays a startup cost. An effective startup cost \(\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) contributes approximately \(2\LayerCount\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) per step before overlap, or \(3\LayerCount\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) with resharding, so FSDP is more sensitive to \(\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) than an unbucketed data-parallel reduction even though its bytes barely change. The counts describe one grouping policy, since FSDP units need not coincide with layers and bucketed data parallelism also splits its reduction to enable overlap. FSDP implementations mitigate the startup cost through prefetching and communication-stream pipelining, but the relevant \(\CollectiveCallStartupLatency\) must come from a matched collective benchmark rather than a wire-latency specification.

↩︎ The reshard-after-forward ZeRO-3/FSDP schedule and its \(1.5\times\) communication volume relative to DDP are established in Memory-efficient data parallelism with ZeRO and FSDP.

Selecting the right collective algorithm at each invocation requires distinguishing transfer cost from dependency exposure. An FSDP AllGather is latency-sensitive in the scheduling sense, because a layer cannot compute until its full parameters arrive. Its transfer can nevertheless be bandwidth-bound when the parameter tensor is large. Prefetching can hide part of that gather, while a ReduceScatter can overlap with the remaining backward computation. Neither dependency alone selects Ring or Tree; message size, group size, topology, and the available overlap window jointly determine the useful schedule.

The primitives specify what must be communicated, and sharding shows how often a training step invokes them. The remaining question is how to execute them efficiently on real networks. The most performance-critical primitive, AllReduce, admits multiple algorithmic implementations whose latency and bandwidth characteristics differ sharply. For the 70B gradient across 1,000 ranks, a simple tree that forwards the whole tensor at every level (the schedule section 6.6.4 examines) pays about \(2\log_2 1000 \approx 20\) full-message transfers against the ring’s two, a tenfold difference in byte time, so this choice can decide whether gradient synchronization can be hidden at all.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why can MoE token routing with AllToAll encounter contention and tail-latency limits even when its per-worker payload is smaller than that of a dense AllReduce?

    1. AllToAll transfers more total bytes per GPU than AllReduce, requiring each GPU to send \(2(N-1)M\) bytes instead of \(2 \frac{N-1}{N} M\) bytes.
    2. AllToAll exchanges distinct payloads among many peers; routing skew and concurrent cross-fabric transfers can concentrate traffic on shared links and destinations.
    3. AllToAll cannot execute over InfiniBand fabrics and must fall back to host CPU TCP/IP socket emulation.
    4. AllToAll mandates single-precision FP32 representation, preventing BF16 or FP16 tensor transfers across remote experts.

  2. How does the communication pattern of Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP / ZeRO-3) differ structurally and operationally from standard Distributed Data Parallelism (DDP)?

    1. DDP communicates twice per transformer layer using Point-to-Point Send/Recv, whereas FSDP communicates only once at the end of the step using Broadcast.
    2. FSDP replaces all network communication with CPU host memory paging, eliminating collective communication entirely.
    3. DDP reduces ready gradient buckets, whereas layer-wrapped full-shard FSDP with resharding typically issues \(3N_L\) collectives: forward and backward AllGathers plus backward ReduceScatters, increasing launch-latency sensitivity.
    4. FSDP eliminates AllGather operations by permanently keeping full model parameters in every GPU high-bandwidth memory.

  3. In Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP), when does a parameter AllGather or gradient ReduceScatter add exposed delay to a training step? Explain why the primitive name alone does not identify a latency-bound or bandwidth-bound transfer.

  4. True or False: In a mixture-of-experts (MoE) model where tokens are routed across 8 GPUs using full-duplex InfiniBand NDR (50 GB/s per port), issuing 7 concurrent peer transfers allows the GPU to achieve an aggregate outgoing transmission rate of \(7 \times 50\text{ GB/s} = 350\text{ GB/s}\).

  5. The mathematical identity that an AllReduce can be decomposed into an intra-group ____ phase (where each worker retains a reduced chunk) followed by an ____ phase (where workers circulate and concatenate the reduced chunks) forms the foundational communication mechanism behind both Ring AllReduce and ZeRO-3/FSDP memory sharding.

See Answers →

6.6 AllReduce Algorithms and Topologies

A correct AllReduce must give each of 1,000 GPUs the reduced sum of all 1,000 gradient contributions, subject to the floating-point reduction contract, and the algorithm that delivers it sets the cluster’s scaling ceiling. The naive answer, in which every GPU sends to one central server whose single network link must carry every worker’s gradient, fails first, which makes it the right place to start.

6.6.1 Naive approaches vs. the bandwidth bottleneck

Consider a naive implementation using a Parameter Server (Star topology), with a dedicated server in addition to the workers. All \(\DeviceCount\) workers send their gradients to that server; it sums them and sends the result back. The server must receive \(\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize\) bytes and send \(\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize\) bytes, so its bandwidth budget grows as \(\CommunicationTime \propto \DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize/ \LinkBandwidth\). At 1,000 GPUs, concentrating those transfers at one server creates a scaling bottleneck even if the arithmetic is fast.

This naive approach captures the pressure that early distributed ML frameworks had to manage: parameter-server systems receive, aggregate, and redistribute model updates through a server tier, while practical implementations shard the parameter space to spread that load (Dean et al. 2012; Li et al. 2014). Sharding reduces the per-server traffic from \(\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize\) to \(\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize/\ParameterServerCount\) (where \(\ParameterServerCount\) is the number of servers), but the server tier still grows as workers and model state grow, and it introduces additional complexity in parameter partitioning and consistency management.

Dean, Jeffrey, Greg Corrado, Rajat Monga, Kai Chen, Matthieu Devin, Quoc V. Le, Mark Z. Mao, et al. 2012. “Large Scale Distributed Deep Networks.” In Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS), edited by Peter L. Bartlett, Fernando C. N. Pereira, Christopher J. C. Burges, Léon Bottou, and Kilian Q. Weinberger, vol. 25. Curran Associates.
Li, M., D. G. Andersen, J. W. Park, A. J. Smola, A. Ahmed, V. Josifovski, J. Long, E. J. Shekita, and B.-Y. Su. 2014. “Scaling Distributed Machine Learning with the Parameter Server.” Proceedings of the 2014 International Conference on Big Data Science and Computing, 583–98. https://doi.org/10.1145/2640087.2644155.

The bottleneck comes from concentration, not from total traffic. Every AllReduce algorithm moves more total data as workers are added, because every worker’s contribution must be combined and every worker must receive the result. A star, however, routes all of that traffic through one endpoint, so the server’s time grows as \(\DeviceCount\times \CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\), while a ring spreads the same work so that each rank sends about \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) and its time stays near \(2\CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\) however large \(\DeviceCount\) becomes. The ratio of the two is about \(\DeviceCount/2\), so at 1,024 workers the server’s byte time is roughly 500\(\times\) the ring’s, before startup, arithmetic, or contention are counted. Bandwidth optimality makes that comparison precise by measuring an algorithm’s required byte movement against a lower bound under a stated communication model, which the next derivation supplies for AllReduce.

War Story 6.1: When the parameter server became the bottleneck (2017)
Context: In 2017, Alexander Sergeev and Mike Del Balso’s team at Uber open-sourced Horovod as part of Uber’s Michelangelo ML platform, where TensorFlow training jobs needed to scale across many GPUs without forcing every model author to rewrite large portions of training code (Sergeev and Balso 2018).

Mechanism: TensorFlow’s stock distributed training relied on a parameter-server pattern that concentrated gradient traffic through a central tier. As worker count \(\DeviceCount\) grew, the central server link saturated, degrading scaling efficiency.

Impact: Scaling training beyond a single node resulted in severe throughput bottlenecks and required complex, model-specific parameter partitioning.

Fix: Uber adopted Baidu’s ring-allreduce algorithm (Gibiansky 2017), wrapping bandwidth-optimal collective reduction behind a lightweight API that replaced parameter servers with ring communication.

Systems lesson: A collective algorithm is also a developer interface. Scaling improves when the communication primitive is both bandwidth efficient and easy enough that teams actually use it correctly.

6.6.2 The bandwidth-optimal lower bound

Every specific algorithm can be measured against a lower bound for point-to-point AllReduce with an associative, commutative reduction and no in-network reduction or compression. Reducing one independent data item across \(\DeviceCount\) ranks and distributing its result requires at least \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\) item transfers in aggregate (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009). The count has two halves. Combining \(\DeviceCount\) contributions that start on \(\DeviceCount\) different ranks takes at least \(\DeviceCount-1\) transfers, because each transfer combines at most two partial sums and so reduces the number of separate partial sums by at most one. Delivering the finished value to the other \(\DeviceCount-1\) ranks then takes at least one transfer each. For an \(\CollectivePayloadSize\)-byte tensor, the minimum aggregate sent volume is therefore \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize\) bytes. At least one rank must send the average share, \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) bytes; a balanced algorithm assigns that share to every rank.

Take \(\LinkBandwidth\) as each rank’s available send bandwidth, with equal receive bandwidth that can operate simultaneously. Dividing the required sent volume by \(\LinkBandwidth\) gives the full-duplex bandwidth lower bound:

\[\AllReduceMinimumBandwidthTime= \frac{2(\DeviceCount-1)}{\DeviceCount} \cdot \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}\]

As \(\DeviceCount\) grows large, the bandwidth lower bound approaches \(2\CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\), independent of \(\DeviceCount\). Ring AllReduce matches the required byte volume under this model; startup and local reduction still add time (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009; Thakur et al. 2005). On a 64-GPU cluster synchronizing the chapter’s 1 GB reference buffer over 50 GB/s links, the ring’s byte term is about 39.4 ms. The simple full-message tree, which forwards the whole tensor once per level on the way up and once per level on the way down (the schedule section 6.6.4 develops), pays \(2\log_2 64 = 12\) full-message transfers, about 240 ms. The byte-term difference of roughly 200 ms per step would accumulate to about 5.6 hours over a 100,000-step run if fully exposed. Actual wall-clock savings also depend on startup, placement, and overlap.

6.6.3 Ring AllReduce

Ring AllReduce8 arranges nodes in a logical ring (\(0 \to 1 \to \dots \to \DeviceCount-1 \to 0\)). Each node sends to its successor while receiving from its predecessor. The chunk schedule distributes the required byte volume evenly across ranks, achieving the bandwidth lower bound in the ideal endpoint model (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009).

8 Bandwidth-Optimal: Under the point-to-point model above, Ring AllReduce sends \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) bytes per rank and receives the same amount. With simultaneous full-duplex transfers at bandwidth \(\LinkBandwidth\) in each direction, its bandwidth time is \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/(\DeviceCount\LinkBandwidth)\). Adding send and receive bytes would double-count elapsed time.

The algorithm splits the vector of size \(\CollectivePayloadSize\) into \(\DeviceCount\) chunks and then alternates communication and local reduction in two phases, a ReduceScatter phase (often called scatter-reduce) followed by an AllGather phase. Algorithm 1 states the mechanics before the figure and trace make the same dataflow concrete.

\(N\)

Across the \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\) send/receive rounds each rank moves \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) bytes per round, for \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) bytes sent and the same received. The bandwidth term therefore reaches the information-theoretic lower bound, while the latency term grows as \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\MessageStartupLatency\). Figure 5 traces this two-phase state progression across a four-GPU ring with wrap-around links. In row (a), \(\DeviceCount-1 = 3\) ReduceScatter steps circulate gradient chunks A through D until each GPU holds exactly one fully reduced partition (green \(\Glyph{\Sigma} \Glyph{A}\) through \(\Glyph{\Sigma} \Glyph{D}\)). In row (b), \(\DeviceCount-1 = 3\) subsequent AllGather steps forward those finished shards around the ring until every device holds the complete sum.

In the ideal schedule in figure 5, every logical ring edge carries a chunk in each round. Each node sends exactly \(2(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\cdot \CollectivePayloadSize\) bytes across both phases, matching the stated byte-volume lower bound. Logical edges may share physical links, however, and startup, local reduction, or contention can leave transfer capacity unused. Byte optimality does not guarantee peak physical-link utilization.

To make the ring algorithm concrete, consider a two-phase example with 4 GPUs reducing a vector of 4 elements by summation. Each GPU \(\RankIndex\) starts with a local gradient vector \(\LocalGradient_i = [\FirstRingTraceElement_i, \SecondRingTraceElement_i, \ThirdRingTraceElement_i, \FourthRingTraceElement_i]\). The vector is split into 4 chunks (one per GPU), and the algorithm proceeds through two phases.

Example 6.1: Ring AllReduce: Two-phase example (4 GPUs)
Scenario: Four GPUs (\(0 \to 1 \to 2 \to 3 \to 0\)) perform a Ring AllReduce on local vectors \([1, 5, 3, 7]\), \([2, 6, 4, 8]\), \([3, 7, 5, 9]\), and \([4, 8, 6, 10]\) to compute the element-wise sum \([10, 26, 18, 34]\).

Diagnosis: The trace has to show three properties of the schedule, namely that it takes \(2(\DeviceCount-1) = 6\) steps, that every GPU sends and receives in every step, and that each step moves only one chunk. Ring AllReduce splits each vector into \(\DeviceCount=4\) chunks \(\FirstRingTraceElement\), \(\SecondRingTraceElement\), \(\ThirdRingTraceElement\), \(\FourthRingTraceElement\). In ReduceScatter step \(\RingStepIndex\) (\(\RingStepIndex= 1, 2, 3\)), GPU \(\RankIndex\) sends its running copy of chunk \((\RankIndex-\RingStepIndex+1) \bmod 4\) to GPU \((\RankIndex+1) \bmod 4\), which adds it to its own copy. After the third step, each GPU owns one fully reduced chunk. In AllGather step \(\RingStepIndex\), GPU \(\RankIndex\) forwards the finished chunk it completed or received most recently to GPU \((\RankIndex+1) \bmod 4\), which stores it without arithmetic. Table 5 records, for all six steps, the chunk each GPU receives and the value it then holds.

Every cell in every row is filled, so every GPU receives (and, symmetrically, sends) one chunk in every step, and after the sixth step every GPU holds [10, 26, 18, 34].

Systems lesson: Ring AllReduce achieves the optimal byte volume under the stated model: each GPU sends \(2\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount}\CollectivePayloadSize\) total bytes, approaching \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) as \(\DeviceCount\) grows, while the startup-round count continues to grow. Physical-link utilization also depends on placement and runtime overhead.

Checkpoint 6.2: Ring AllReduce mechanics

Verify your understanding of bandwidth-optimal reduction:

The performance follows directly from the two-phase schedule in example 6.1. Each node sends and receives \(\frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\DeviceCount}\) bytes in each of the \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\) steps. \[ \RingAllReduceCompletionTime = \underbrace{2(\DeviceCount-1)\MessageStartupLatency}_{\text{Latency Term}} + \underbrace{2\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount} \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}}_{\text{Bandwidth Term}} \]

The bandwidth term approaches \(2\CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\) as \(\DeviceCount\to \infty\), reaching the full-duplex lower bound: each rank sends nearly \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) bytes across the two phases and receives the same amount concurrently. In contrast, the latency term scales linearly with \(\DeviceCount\). Across 10,000 nodes, 20,000 sequential hops accumulate substantial latency, which explains why Ring AllReduce becomes uncompetitive for small payloads.

Napkin Math 6.4: AllReduce cost for a 70B model
Problem: Consider a communication-only baseline for a 70B parameter model across 64 GPUs connected by InfiniBand NDR (50 GB/s per port). The bandwidth term barely depends on rank count, so this smaller group reproduces the bandwidth-only estimate from the opening definition while keeping the latency term visible. Each rank contributes a complete logical gradient buffer communicated in BF16 (2 bytes per parameter). This calculation isolates collective cost without establishing that the training state fits on each GPU. How long does one AllReduce take?

Step 1: Size the gradient payload. Each rank in this baseline contributes the complete logical gradient tensor of \(7 \times 10^{10} \times 2\ \text{bytes}\) \(=\) 140 GB. A hybrid layout that partitions the model would instead reduce corresponding gradient shards within smaller groups and requires its own calculation.

Step 2: Apply the Ring AllReduce bandwidth formula. \[\RingAllReduceBandwidthTime= 2 \cdot \frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount} \cdot \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}\]

Substituting: \(\RingAllReduceBandwidthTime\) = 1.96875 \(\times\) 140 GB / 50 GB/s \(\approx\) 5,512.5 ms.

Step 3: Add the latency term. \[\RingAllReduceStartupTime= 2(\DeviceCount-1) \cdot \MessageStartupLatency\]

Substituting: \(\RingAllReduceStartupTime=\) 126 \(\times\) 1.5 μs = 0.2 ms.

Step 4: Total communication time. Total: \(\RingAllReduceCompletionTime \approx\) 5,512.5 ms + 0.2 ms \(\approx\) 5,512.7 ms.

Systems insight: The modeled full-buffer AllReduce takes over five seconds under the stated link and rank assumptions, and almost all of it is the bandwidth term. Its effect on step time depends on how much remains exposed relative to useful computation. When it is fully exposed, halving it saves about 2.8 s per step; across a 100,000-step campaign on these 64 GPUs, that is roughly 4,900 GPU-hours of reserved accelerator time. Large-scale training systems can launch reductions for output-side gradients while input-side layers are still computing. Partitioned training also changes each rank’s payload and participating group, so this complete-gradient budget must be recomputed for the actual layout.

6.6.4 Tree AllReduce

Ring AllReduce’s byte efficiency comes with many startup rounds. As the group grows, the \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\MessageStartupLatency\) term can exceed the byte-transfer term for a fixed payload; the crossover depends on the measured path parameters. Tree AllReduce reduces the number of dependent rounds. In the simple reduce phase, pairs combine contributions and forward partial sums toward a root, taking \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) steps for a balanced power-of-two group. The broadcast phase distributes the result in another \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) steps. This shorter dependency chain motivates the comparison before bandwidth costs are counted.

Ring latency grows with N; tree stays logarithmic.

The simple full-message tree loses parallel activity as contributions converge. A ring activates all \(\DeviceCount\) logical edges in each round. In a pairwise tree reduction, the first round has \(\DeviceCount/2\) senders and \(\DeviceCount/2\) receivers; receiving is useful work, not idleness. The next round has \(\DeviceCount/4\) senders and \(\DeviceCount/4\) receivers, and the final reduction exchange involves two ranks while the remaining \((\DeviceCount-2)\) have completed their upstream contribution. Sending the full message through successive levels adds byte-transfer time along the dependency path. This is a limitation of the illustrated schedule, not every tree-based implementation or every physical link mapping.

The resulting time complexity depends on the specific tree variant. A simple reduce-then-broadcast tree has logarithmic latency but nonuniform traffic and root/interior bottlenecks. Recursive doubling, in which partners at doubling distances exchange the entire tensor in each of \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) rounds, has the same logarithmic round count and incurs \(\mathcal{O}(\log \DeviceCount)\) full-message bandwidth per rank. The following simplified model captures the latency advantage and the potential bandwidth penalty of tree-like collectives: \[ \TreeLikeAllReduceTime\approx \underbrace{2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency}_{\text{Latency}} + \underbrace{2 \log_2 \DeviceCount\frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}}_{\text{Bandwidth penalty in full-message exchange variants}} \]

The latency term is logarithmic (\(\mathcal{O}(\log \DeviceCount)\)): for 1024 nodes, Ring needs 2046 steps while Tree needs only 20, and that 100\(\times\) reduction in steps makes Tree attractive for small, frequent collectives across large process groups, such as scalar reductions, control messages, or small-batch activation reductions. The bandwidth term carries the penalty, because tree-like algorithms can underutilize links or concentrate traffic on interior links, and recursive-doubling-style full-message exchanges add a \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) bandwidth factor; these penalties make naive tree variants a poor choice for data parallelism’s large gradient AllReduce, where bandwidth efficiency determines whether the network is saturated or partially idle.

For large-scale data parallelism, this bandwidth penalty decides the comparison whenever the implementation repeatedly exchanges full messages or creates root/interior bottlenecks. In the 64-GPU example of section 6.6.2, the simple tree already pays about 6.1× the ring’s byte time. For a 70B-parameter model’s 140 GB gradient AllReduce across 1,000 GPUs, a bandwidth-inefficient tree-like variant can erase the latency advantage that motivated it. Optimized double-tree algorithms exist precisely to keep logarithmic latency without imposing that naive bandwidth penalty.

Small activation reductions provide a useful case for fewer startup rounds. For an eight-GPU group communicating a 1 MB tensor, the simple model reduces Ring’s \(2(8-1)\MessageStartupLatency= 14\MessageStartupLatency\) to Tree’s \(2\log_2(8)\MessageStartupLatency= 6\MessageStartupLatency\), but Tree’s byte term remains about \(3.4\times\) Ring’s. Whether the saved startups outweigh those extra byte transfers depends on the measured latency and bandwidth.
