Tensor-parallel collectives also have a scheduling constraint independent of this comparison: a layer that needs the reduced activation must wait for it. A critical-path dependency does not by itself imply a latency-dominated transfer or select a tree algorithm.

6.6.5 Recursive halving-doubling (butterfly)

The simple full-message Tree reduces startup rounds but repeats more byte-transfer work than Ring. Recursive halving-doubling, sometimes called the Butterfly algorithm, combines logarithmic rounds with the optimal byte volume under the flat-network model. It pairs ranks exactly as recursive doubling does, but where recursive doubling exchanges the whole tensor in every round, halving-doubling halves the exchanged region in each ReduceScatter round and doubles it in each AllGather round, and that difference removes the \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) byte penalty. For a power-of-two group, it uses \(2\log_2 \DeviceCount\) rounds: \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) ReduceScatter rounds followed by \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) AllGather rounds. A partner-bit index \(\PartnerBitIndex\) selects the XOR pairing with mask \(2^\PartnerBitIndex\). The ReduceScatter trace below visits those bits in increasing order as messages shrink; the matching AllGather reverses that order as the assembled result grows.

In the ReduceScatter phase (first \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) rounds), GPU \(\RankIndex\) partners with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 2^\PartnerBitIndex\) (XOR of indices). The partners retain complementary halves of the current tensor region and send the other half. Each adds the received contribution to the matching half it retained. After \(\log_2 \DeviceCount\) rounds, each GPU owns \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\) bytes of the fully reduced result. AllGather traverses the partner bits in reverse, exchanging completed regions and doubling the data held at each rank until every rank has the full result.

The performance of Recursive Halving-Doubling is: \[ \ButterflyAllReduceTime= 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency+ 2\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount} \cdot \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \]

This achieves the best of both worlds: logarithmic latency (\(\mathcal{O}(\log \DeviceCount)\), like Tree) and bandwidth-optimal data movement (\(2(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\cdot \CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\), like Ring). The catch is that it requires nonneighbor communication (GPU \(\RankIndex\) must communicate with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 2^\PartnerBitIndex\), which may be physically distant), and it requires \(\DeviceCount\) to be a power of two. For clusters where \(\DeviceCount\) is not a power of two, additional complexity is needed to handle the irregular cases.

Recursive halving-doubling is used in MPI-style collective implementations and is useful as a theoretical point in the latency-bandwidth trade-off (Thakur et al. 2005). NVIDIA’s GPU collective backend, the NVIDIA Collective Communications Library (NCCL), offers implementation families that include ring, tree-derived, hierarchical, NVLink/NVSwitch-aware, and pattern-aware choices, with exact names and availability changing by version and topology (Jeaugey 2017; NVIDIA 2026). Whatever a particular release calls them, practical communication libraries select algorithms through a topology-aware cost model because nonlocal communication patterns can create contention on shared network links that erodes theoretical advantages.

Thakur, Rajeev, Rolf Rabenseifner, and William Gropp. 2005. “Optimization of Collective Communication Operations in MPICH.” The International Journal of High Performance Computing Applications 19 (1): 49–66. https://doi.org/10.1177/1094342005051521.

The partner distance is where topology enters. Consider the ReduceScatter phase for 8 GPUs placed four per node on two nodes, GPUs 0–3 on the first and GPUs 4–7 on the second, which proceeds in \(\log_2(8) = 3\) rounds. In round \(\PartnerBitIndex=0\), each GPU \(\RankIndex\) partners with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 1\), pairing neighbors: (0,1), (2,3), (4,5), (6,7). They exchange half their data and reduce. In round \(\PartnerBitIndex=1\), the distance doubles: GPU \(\RankIndex\) partners with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 2\), creating pairs (0,2), (1,3), (4,6), (5,7). In round \(\PartnerBitIndex=2\), the distance doubles again: GPU \(\RankIndex\) partners with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 4\), creating pairs (0,4), (1,5), (2,6), (3,7).

Physical placement determines the cost of these logical pairings. Rounds \(\PartnerBitIndex=0\) and \(\PartnerBitIndex=1\) pair GPUs within a node, but in round \(\PartnerBitIndex=2\) every GPU on the first node exchanges with one on the second, so that round crosses the node boundary. On eight-GPU nodes the same crossing would appear one round later, at \(\PartnerBitIndex=3\) in a 16-rank group. In a separate 1,024-rank example, the final ReduceScatter round pairs GPU \(\RankIndex\) with GPU \(\RankIndex\oplus 512\). If the two rank halves occupy different racks, those exchanges share the inter-rack fabric. The algorithm’s logarithmic rounds and optimal byte volume therefore do not establish its runtime advantage; placement, shared paths, and the changing chunk size in each round must enter the comparison.

6.6.6 Double binary tree

NCCL can use a double binary tree for many message sizes and topologies, which addresses the bandwidth inefficiency of a standard binary tree while allowing topology-aware tree placement (Jeaugey 2017; NVIDIA 2026). The idea is to construct two independent binary trees with complementary interior-node roles, then run both trees simultaneously, each carrying half the data.

The complementary-tree construction balances endpoint work by making ranks that act as interior nodes in one tree act as leaves in the other. With eight ranks, build the first tree by labeling the nodes of a binary tree in the order of an in-order traversal (left subtree, then node, then right subtree). In a balanced seven-node tree labeled from 0, in-order traversal puts the leaves at the even ranks 0, 2, 4, and 6 and the interior nodes at 1, 3, and 5; in one simple construction the eighth rank, 7, joins as one more interior node above that tree. The odd ranks 1, 3, 5, and 7 thus form the first tree’s interior and the even ranks are its leaves; the second tree shifts every rank by one position, so the even ranks become its interior and the odd ranks its leaves. Each tree carries half the tensor, \(\CollectivePayloadSize/2\), and the two halves never meet.

Two further steps bring the byte term down from the simple tree’s \(2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\) to about \(2\CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\). First, each tree pipelines its half in small chunks, so while one chunk moves up from the leaves, the next is already leaving them and every level works at once; tree depth then adds startup rounds and a short pipeline fill, not repeated full-message transfers. Second, count what one rank sends during the reduce phase. As an interior node of one tree it combines the inputs of its (at most two) children and forwards one \(\CollectivePayloadSize/2\) stream to its parent, and as a leaf of the other tree it sends its own \(\CollectivePayloadSize/2\) contribution, so it sends about \(\CollectivePayloadSize\) in total. The broadcast phase mirrors that traffic, for about \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) per rank across both phases, the same per-rank volume as the ring. This logical balance does not guarantee that every physical link stays busy or that both trees avoid shared fabric bottlenecks; rank placement and routing still matter.

The combined performance is approximately: \[ \DoubleTreeAllReduceTime\approx 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \]

The ideal bandwidth term approaches \(2\CollectivePayloadSize/\LinkBandwidth\) while the startup-round count remains logarithmic. This combination makes Double Binary Tree a useful candidate across message sizes that penalize either the ring’s many rounds or the simple tree’s repeated full-message transfers. Complementary endpoint roles do not ensure disjoint physical paths, so topology-aware tree construction seeks a good placement, and measurements must establish the achieved rate under contention.

6.6.7 The algorithm crossover point

Plotting the three cost models for a 256-GPU group on a 200 Gbps fabric, over message sizes from 1 KB to 10 GB, shows the simple Tree and Ring curves intersecting near 1 MB (figure 6). At small sizes, fewer startup rounds favor the tree schedules; at large sizes, the simple full-message Tree pays more byte-transfer time. The Double Binary Tree curve includes illustrative overhead factors, so it approaches a comparable bandwidth-dominated regime without representing a measured library result. Table 6 summarizes the assumptions behind these candidate costs.

The intersection near 1 MB in figure 6 marks where the modeled Ring and simple Tree have equal total cost. It is an algorithm crossover, distinct from the startup-versus-transfer crossover for one message. Setting \(\RingAllReduceCompletionTime = \TreeAllReduceCompletionTime\) and solving for payload \(\CollectivePayloadSize\) identifies the point where Ring’s lower byte cost compensates for its additional startup rounds:

\[ \RingAllReduceCompletionTime = 2(\DeviceCount-1)\MessageStartupLatency+ 2\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount}\frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \] \[ \TreeAllReduceCompletionTime = 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency+ 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \]

For large \(\DeviceCount\), \((\DeviceCount-1) \approx \DeviceCount\) and \(\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount} \approx 1\), so the ring pays a latency term proportional to \(\DeviceCount\) while moving each byte close to the bandwidth limit:

\[ \RingAllReduceCompletionTime \approx 2\DeviceCount\MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}, \quad \TreeAllReduceCompletionTime \approx 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \]

The tree has the opposite shape: logarithmic startup cost, but each message byte moves through more stages. Setting the estimates equal isolates the message size where the cheaper latency path stops compensating for the extra bandwidth work:

\[ 2\DeviceCount\MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} = 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency+ \frac{2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \] \[ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} - \frac{2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} = 2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency- 2\DeviceCount\MessageStartupLatency\] \[ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}(1 - \log_2 \DeviceCount) = 2\MessageStartupLatency(\log_2 \DeviceCount- \DeviceCount) \]

Since \(\DeviceCount\gg \log_2 \DeviceCount\) for large clusters, \((\log_2 \DeviceCount- \DeviceCount) \approx -\DeviceCount\) and \((1 - \log_2 \DeviceCount) \approx -\log_2 \DeviceCount\), yielding:

\[ \frac{2\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth}(-\log_2 \DeviceCount) \approx 2\MessageStartupLatency(-\DeviceCount) \] \[ \boxed{\RingTreeCrossoverPayload \approx \frac{\DeviceCount\cdot \MessageStartupLatency\cdot \LinkBandwidth}{\log_2 \DeviceCount}} \]

The log-aware crossover formula is the large-\(\DeviceCount\) approximation to the exact crossover \(\RingTreeCrossoverPayload(\DeviceCount)\) derived in Ring vs. tree vs. recursive halving-doubling. Within this two-algorithm model, Ring wins above the crossover because its byte savings exceed its extra startup cost; the simple Tree wins below it because the startup saving is larger. Optimized trees and topology-dependent rates require separate comparisons.

On the reference cluster, with the chapter’s effective NDR \(\MessageStartupLatency=\) 1.5 μs and \(\LinkBandwidth=\) 50 GB/s across all 1,024 GPUs, the estimate gives \(\RingTreeCrossoverPayload \approx\) \(\frac{1{,}024 \times 1.5\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s} \times 50\ \text{GB/s}}{\log_2 1{,}024} = \frac{1{,}024 \times 1.5\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s} \times 50\ \text{GB/s}}{10}\) \(\approx\) 7.7 MB. A gradient bucket of tens of megabytes or the full model gradient therefore sits on the ring side of the crossover, while a reduction of a few hundred kilobytes sits on the tree side. The two other configurations in this section depart from that anchor in stated ways. Figure 6 uses a smaller 256-GPU group on a slower 200 Gbps fabric with \(\MessageStartupLatency= 1\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\), which moves the curves’ intersection down to about 1 MB, and notebook 6.5 uses a 64-GPU group on a still slower, higher-latency fabric, where a 1 MB buffer lands almost exactly on the crossover.

In practice, NCCL’s algorithm selection is more nuanced than a simple two-way split. The Double Binary Tree algorithm offers logarithmic latency with near-optimal bandwidth, making it competitive with both Ring and Tree across a broad range of message sizes. NCCL also considers the number of channels (parallel communication streams), the network topology, and whether inter-node or intra-node links are being used. The effective selection logic resembles a multi-way decision tree indexed by (message size, GPU count, topology type) rather than a single crossover point.

Nevertheless, the crossover formula remains the essential mental model for understanding why libraries make the choices they do. When a library selects an unexpected algorithm, the crossover analysis provides the reasoning framework to evaluate whether the choice is correct or whether manual override is warranted.

Training frameworks decide which payloads reach that analysis. PyTorch DistributedDataParallel (DDP) groups gradient tensors into buckets (section 6.10.2), while FSDP groups parameter gathers and gradient reductions according to its sharding units. A bucket size alone does not locate a crossover, because rank count, effective startup cost, and available bandwidth also enter the comparison, so NCCL’s algorithm selection must be evaluated for the actual invocation and topology rather than inferred from a nominal bucket-size range. A single 1 MB buffer on a slower fabric shows how close to the crossover a real bucket can sit.

Napkin Math 6.5: The ring vs. tree crossover
Problem: A cluster synchronizes a 1 MB buffer across 64 GPUs. The network has latency \(\MessageStartupLatency=\) 10 μs and bandwidth \(\LinkBandwidth=\) 10 GB/s. Which algorithm performs better: Ring or Tree?

Math:

Latency:

  1. Ring Latency: \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\MessageStartupLatency=\) \(2 \times 63 \times 10\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) = 1260 μs.
  2. Tree Latency: \(2(\log_2 \DeviceCount)\MessageStartupLatency=\) \(2 \times 6 \times 10\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) = 120 μs.

Bandwidth (note the difference):

  1. Ring Bandwidth: \(2\frac{\DeviceCount-1}{\DeviceCount}\frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} \approx\) \(2 \times \frac{1\text{ MB}}{10\text{ GB/s}}\) = 196.9 μs (optimal: each rank sends about \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\) in total, the lower bound).
  2. Tree Bandwidth: \(2\log_2 \DeviceCount\cdot \frac{\CollectivePayloadSize}{\LinkBandwidth} =\) \(12 \times \frac{1\text{ MB}}{10\text{ GB/s}}\) = 1200 μs (each level sends full message).

Table 7 sums the latency and bandwidth contributions into a head-to-head comparison:

Tree wins, but only by 9 percent. For this 1 MB message, the payload is near the crossover point.

Systems insight: Ring’s latency penalty (10\(\times\) worse than Tree) nearly balances Tree’s bandwidth penalty (6\(\times\) worse than Ring). The log-aware crossover estimate predicts \(\RingTreeCrossoverPayload \approx \DeviceCount\MessageStartupLatency\LinkBandwidth/ \log_2 \DeviceCount= 64 \times 10\ \MicroUnitPrefix\text{s} \times 10\ \text{GB/s} / 6 \approx\) 1.1 MB. At 1 MB, the payload is just below crossover, so Tree wins. At 10 MB, Ring would dominate.

The crossover point sits inside the range that gradient reductions can traverse as bucket sizes change. In this example, the 1 MB AllReduce lies near crossover, whereas a 10 MB DDP bucket is already above it. A fused bucket of 50–300 MB or the full 140 GB BF16 gradient of a 70B parameters model favors Ring over the simple Tree model even more strongly. These conclusions apply to the stated AllReduce configuration. An MoE token-routing payload of the same size still requires an AllToAll schedule, and optimized tree or hierarchical implementations require their own cost comparison.

The crossover analysis makes algorithm selection a comparison under specified conditions. Communication libraries such as NCCL use tuning logic that accounts for message size, rank count, topology, and protocol. The simple model can identify a candidate worth benchmarking, but an apparent disagreement with the library is not proof that its choice is wrong. Compare measured collective duration and exposed training-step time before retaining an override.

Checkpoint 6.3: AllReduce algorithm selection

Verify your understanding of Ring vs. Tree AllReduce trade-offs:

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why does Ring AllReduce achieve the information-theoretic lower bound for bandwidth (\(2 \frac{N-1}{N} \frac{M}{\beta}\)) while a simple unsegmented binary Tree AllReduce incurs a bandwidth penalty of \(\mathcal{O}(\log N \cdot \frac{M}{\beta})\)?

    1. Ring AllReduce distributes uniform chunks (\(M/N\)) across all logical ring edges in each round, whereas the simple unsegmented tree sends full messages through successive levels of a reduction and broadcast.
    2. Ring AllReduce compresses floating-point gradients to 1-bit representations during ring circulation, whereas Tree AllReduce always uses uncompressed FP64 precision.
    3. Tree AllReduce requires an external parameter server node to perform floating-point additions, whereas Ring AllReduce computes sums purely in network switch ASICs.
    4. Ring AllReduce eliminates the latency term \(\alpha\) entirely by using asynchronous token rings, whereas Tree AllReduce scales latency quadratically with cluster size.

  2. An infrastructure engineer evaluates AllReduce algorithm selection for synchronizing gradients across a 256-GPU cluster with per-node launch latency \(\alpha = 5\,\mu\text{s}\) and link bandwidth \(\beta = 50\text{ GB/s}\). Using the log-aware crossover model \(M_{\text{crossover}} \approx \frac{N \alpha \beta}{\log_2 N}\), within the simple full-message Tree versus Ring comparison, what is the crossover message size, and which modeled candidate has lower cost for a 500 KB activation tensor versus a 100 MB gradient bucket?

    1. The crossover is \(M_{\text{crossover}} \approx 1.25\text{ GB}\); Tree AllReduce should be selected for both tensors because \(\log_2(256) = 8\) is always smaller than 256.
    2. The crossover is \(M_{\text{crossover}} \approx 800\text{ KB}\); Ring AllReduce should be selected for 500 KB, and Tree AllReduce for 100 MB.
    3. The crossover is \(M_{\text{crossover}} \approx 16\text{ GB}\); Butterfly AllReduce should be selected for both tensors regardless of network topology.
    4. The crossover is \(M_{\text{crossover}} \approx \frac{256 \times (5 \times 10^{-6}\text{ s}) \times (50 \times 10^9\text{ B/s})}{8} = 8\text{ MB}\); Tree AllReduce wins for the 500 KB tensor (latency-dominated), while Ring AllReduce wins for the 100 MB bucket (bandwidth-dominated).

  3. Recursive halving-doubling (Butterfly AllReduce) combines logarithmic startup rounds with bandwidth-optimal byte volume in a flat-network model. What physical placement condition can defeat that modeled advantage, and why do double-tree or hierarchical alternatives still require measurement?

  4. Explain the structural mechanism of the Double Binary Tree AllReduce algorithm that allows it to achieve near-optimal bandwidth (\(\approx 2M/\beta\)) while preserving logarithmic latency (\(\mathcal{O}(\log N)\alpha\)).

  5. Sequence the execution steps of a 4-GPU Ring AllReduce (\(0 \to 1 \to 2 \to 3 \to 0\)) reducing a 4-chunk vector \([A, B, C, D]\): (1) each GPU forwards its fully reduced chunk around the ring for \(N-1\) steps until all ranks possess the complete array, (2) rank \(i\) owns a single fully aggregated chunk of the global sum, (3) each GPU partitions its local vector into \(N=4\) equal chunks, (4) each GPU simultaneously sends chunk \(k\) to its right neighbor and receives from its left neighbor, accumulating partial sums over \(N-1\) steps.

  6. Under the standard balanced point-to-point model, an AllReduce with \(M\) bytes per rank has a per-rank sent-volume lower bound of \(2(N-1)M/N\) bytes. Meeting this bound is known as ____ optimality, which Ring AllReduce achieves.

See Answers →

6.7 Hierarchical Communication

The AllReduce cost models in section 6.6 assume a flat network with uniform endpoint bandwidth. A multi-node GPU cluster instead has distinct local and cross-node communication paths. The DGX H100 nodes of the reference cluster show the gap at its starkest, with each GPU’s NVLink capacity roughly nine times its one InfiniBand port, the bandwidth hierarchy that Bandwidth hierarchy established.

Hierarchical communication is the response to that inequality. Intra-node links are abundant and fast, inter-node links are scarce and expensive, and the algorithm must shrink payloads before they cross the scarce tier whenever possible. This is why the same AllReduce can be implemented as a local ReduceScatter, a cross-node reduction, and a local AllGather rather than as one flat exchange across every rank.

6.7.1 Hierarchical AllReduce

On a DGX H100 node, each GPU’s local NVLink capacity is 450 GB/s per direction, while its inter-node port provides 50 GB/s over InfiniBand. These are endpoint capacity budgets, not a promise of uniform achieved rate to every peer. Intra-node connectivity and simultaneous traffic still matter. The hierarchical network in table 8 motivates a schedule that assigns work to each tier according to its usable capacity.

A flat ring whose ordering repeatedly crosses node boundaries can leave local NVLink capacity unused while loading scarce inter-node links. Hierarchical AllReduce explicitly partitions the global operation into three stages that align communication volume with the fabric tiers (figure 7).

By decoupling local aggregation from global synchronization, figure 7 stages the dataflow so that slower inter-node InfiniBand links carry traffic only during Phase 2 (Shard AllReduce). Write \(\AcceleratorsPerNode\) for the GPUs per node and \(\ClusterNodeCount\) for the number of nodes. In this middle phase, each corresponding-rank group reduces an \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\AcceleratorsPerNode\)-byte shard across \(\ClusterNodeCount\) nodes. A ring in that group sends \(2(\ClusterNodeCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/(\ClusterNodeCount\AcceleratorsPerNode)\) bytes per participating GPU and receives the same amount. Summing across the node’s \(\AcceleratorsPerNode\) GPUs gives \(2(\ClusterNodeCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/\ClusterNodeCount\) bytes sent per node. Smaller per-port payloads improve elapsed time when the node has independent ports and sufficient fabric capacity; sharding does not create bandwidth on a shared uplink.

The three phases form a single causal pipeline. First, an intra-node ReduceScatter over NVLink performs a local reduction among the \(\AcceleratorsPerNode\) GPUs in each node, leaving each GPU with a \(1/\AcceleratorsPerNode\) shard of partially reduced data while consuming only abundant intra-node bandwidth. Next, each corresponding-rank group applies an inter-node AllReduce over InfiniBand to its \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\AcceleratorsPerNode\)-byte input shard, where the chosen collective algorithm dictates the bytes transmitted across the network fabric. Finally, an intra-node AllGather over NVLink redistributes the globally reduced shards within each node, completing the collective without consuming additional inter-node bandwidth.

A simple 8-node budget makes this bandwidth multiplication concrete:

Napkin Math 6.6: The hierarchical bandwidth multiplier
Problem: A cluster has 8 nodes, each with 8 GPUs (64 total). The system must AllReduce the chapter’s 1 GB reference buffer. Assume each GPU has its own full-rate inter-node port, one rail per GPU as on a DGX H100 (rails are the subject of section 6.7.4.1), with no oversubscription. The flat baseline is a single ring over all 64 ranks. How much faster is Hierarchical AllReduce than that flat ring?

Flat ring AllReduce (ignoring hierarchy):

  • Each GPU sends ~2 GB total (the bandwidth-optimal Ring AllReduce formula).
  • Every edge of a ring carries the same traffic in every step, so the whole ring advances at the rate of its slowest edge. Every ordering puts at least one fabric-crossing edge on each node, even the node-contiguous ordering that crosses the fabric only once per node.
  • Effective Bandwidth: Limited by the slowest link = 50 GB/s (InfiniBand).
  • Time: \(\approx\) \(2 \times 1\ \text{GB} / 50\ \text{GB/s}\) = 40 ms (bandwidth term dominates).

Hierarchical AllReduce (3-step decomposition):

  1. Intra-node ReduceScatter: Each GPU sends 875 MB at 450 GB/s per direction → ~1.94 ms

  2. Inter-Node AllReduce: Each GPU AllReduces a \(1\ \text{GB}/8\) = 125 MB shard over InfiniBand; Ring moves roughly \(2(\ClusterNodeCount-1)/\ClusterNodeCount\) times that payload, and after adding the small latency term the phase takes ~4.40 ms at 50 GB/s

    (The eight shard groups run concurrently, each on its own GPU’s port, so the node’s eight ports together carry the full payload in parallel.)

  3. Intra-node AllGather: Each GPU receives 875 MB at 450 GB/s per direction → ~1.94 ms

  4. Total time: \(\approx\) 1.94 ms + 4.40 ms + 1.94 ms = 8.3 ms

Systems insight: Hierarchical AllReduce achieves a 4.8× speedup by reducing the inter-node payload from 1 GB to 125 MB per GPU before the Ring traffic multiplier. The flat ring used one port’s worth of fabric bandwidth because a single ring runs at its slowest edge; the hierarchical schedule uses all eight ports at once. That is also its precondition, since a node whose GPUs shared one uplink would carry the eight shard streams one after another and lose most of the speedup. This is why NVIDIA’s NCCL and similar libraries often select hierarchical algorithms on multi-node clusters when the topology supports them.

The speedup carries over to model-sized payloads, because every phase is linear in bytes. Applied to the chapter’s 140 GB 70B gradient across 128 ranks, the same three phases cut a flat ring’s 5,556.6 ms to about 1,200.7 ms, provided each GPU has its own port for the concurrent shard exchanges.

The volume’s running instance makes the same comparison on the cluster it was defined for. Archetype A’s 175B-parameter model produces a 350 GB BF16 gradient, and the reference cluster holds 1,024 GPUs as 128 nodes of 8. A single flat ring over every GPU would spend about 14.0 s per full-gradient reduction. The hierarchical schedule sends one-eighth of the gradient through each GPU’s port in a shard ring that spans every node and completes in about 3.1 s, a 4.5× improvement, of which the cross-node phase still accounts for about 1.7 s. Seconds per step remain a large exposed cost even after hierarchy, which is why the production layout for this instance shards the model so that each data-parallel group reduces a fraction of this payload, and why the overlap and compression techniques later in the chapter still matter at this scale.

These three phases also generalize to additional fabric tiers. For example, 4 racks of 4 nodes with 8 GPUs per node contain 128 GPUs. A rack-local ReduceScatter over 32 GPUs can leave each GPU with one thirty-second of the original payload for the cross-rack phase. The total elapsed time still includes both local phases, the cross-rack collective’s traffic factor, startup, and contention. A cross-rack oversubscription ratio alone cannot determine a speedup over a flat ring.

6.7.2 Multidimensional reduction on a torus

Hierarchy fits a fabric whose tiers differ in speed. A torus, the direct-connect topology of Google’s Tensor Processing Unit (TPU) pods, has uniform links instead, and its collectives reduce along one dimension at a time. TPU v4 pods support a 3D torus topology, unlike the 2D torus used by TPU v2 and v3. In the regular 3D torus model, each chip links directly to six neighbors \((\pm \Glyph{X}, \pm \Glyph{Y}, \pm \Glyph{Z})\) with equal per-link bandwidth. For collectives, the torus invites dimension-ordered reduction, which decomposes the AllReduce into sequential Ring AllReduces along axis-aligned torus rings. For a pod with \(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount{\times}\TorusSecondAxisChipCount{\times}\TorusThirdAxisChipCount\) chips, ranks first execute independent X-dimension rings for each fixed \((\Glyph{Y},\Glyph{Z})\) coordinate, producing partial sums along each X line. A second phase runs Y-dimension rings to combine those partials across the \(\Glyph{X} \times \Glyph{Y}\) plane, and a third executes Z-dimension rings across fixed \((\Glyph{X},\Glyph{Y})\) coordinates to complete the global reduction.

↩︎ The torus topology and its bisection and path-length trade-offs against the fat-tree are established in Dragonfly and torus alternatives.

Figure 8 maps this dimension-ordered schedule onto a \(4 \times 4\) 2D torus grid (16 ranks). In Phase 1 (orange solid paths), each row executes an independent 4-rank Ring AllReduce across the horizontal X dimension using dashed wrap-around boundary links to close the ring (\(2(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount-1) = 6\) steps). Once row partial sums are established, Phase 2 (slate solid paths) executes orthogonal 4-rank vertical Ring AllReduces down the Y dimension (\(2(\TorusSecondAxisChipCount-1) = 6\) steps). These phases use direct nearest-neighbor links without multi-hop routing within a logical ring edge. They reduce the startup count from \(2(\DeviceCount-1) = 30\) rounds on a flat 16-rank ring to \(2(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount-1) + 2(\TorusSecondAxisChipCount-1) = 12\) rounds, a \(2.5\times\) reduction in the modeled startup term. In this simple form each axis still processes the full message, so the round-count saving comes at a byte cost that the partitioned dimension-wise schedule removes.

The byte term does not shrink with the rings. Because every axis processes the full \(\CollectivePayloadSize\)-byte tensor, the bandwidth time is \(2\CollectivePayloadSize[(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount-1)/\TorusFirstAxisChipCount+ (\TorusSecondAxisChipCount-1)/\TorusSecondAxisChipCount+ (\TorusThirdAxisChipCount-1)/\TorusThirdAxisChipCount]/\LinkBandwidth\) for equal per-axis bandwidth \(\LinkBandwidth\), while the startup term is \(2[(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount-1)+(\TorusSecondAxisChipCount-1)+(\TorusThirdAxisChipCount-1)]\MessageStartupLatency\). Shorter rings reduce startup cost, but these sequential full-message AllReduces do not retain the bandwidth cost of one global ring. A bandwidth-efficient multidimensional implementation instead partitions the work with dimension-wise ReduceScatter followed by reverse-order AllGather. The X-dimension ReduceScatter sends \(\CollectivePayloadSize(\TorusFirstAxisChipCount-1)/\TorusFirstAxisChipCount\) bytes per rank and leaves each rank an \(\CollectivePayloadSize/\TorusFirstAxisChipCount\) shard; the Y-dimension ReduceScatter then operates only on that shard and sends \((\CollectivePayloadSize/\TorusFirstAxisChipCount)(\TorusSecondAxisChipCount-1)/\TorusSecondAxisChipCount\), and the Z dimension continues the pattern. The three terms telescope to \(\CollectivePayloadSize(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\) for \(\DeviceCount= \TorusFirstAxisChipCount \TorusSecondAxisChipCount \TorusThirdAxisChipCount\), and the mirrored AllGather doubles that to \(2(\DeviceCount-1)\CollectivePayloadSize/\DeviceCount\), the ring’s optimal byte term, while keeping the short rings’ startup count. The torus thus teaches the same lesson as hierarchy, which is to shrink the payload before each successive stage rather than send the full tensor through every one.

Dimension-ordered reduction follows direct neighbor links and separates axis traffic in time; it does not change the network diameter or eliminate contention with other workloads. The torus topology also provides natural fault tolerance through alternate routing paths. If one link in the X-dimension fails, traffic can detour through the Y or Z dimensions (at the cost of increased latency). The compiler and collective runtime must choose a schedule for the actual topology, message size, and available links.

6.7.3 In-network reduction with SHARP

Hierarchical and dimension-ordered schedules redistribute work across links, but their software reduction stages still require endpoint GPUs to receive and combine contributions. NVIDIA’s Scalable Hierarchical Aggregation and Reduction Protocol (SHARP)9 moves that intermediate aggregation into the network. Instead of routing each partial sum through a GPU for arithmetic, an aggregation-capable InfiniBand switch combines contributions as packets pass through it.

9 SHARP (Scalable Hierarchical Aggregation and Reduction Protocol): In-network computing on Quantum InfiniBand switches that performs reduction operations (such as sum, min, and max) as packets traverse an aggregation tree (Graham et al. 2020). The same work reports substantially higher reduction bandwidth than host-based algorithms and describes switch-resource limits on outstanding aggregation groups and trees, which can create contention when many collectives compete for the same in-network resources.

Graham, R. L., D. Bureddy, P. Lui, H. Rober, G. Bloch, G. Shainer, J. Poole, et al. 2020. “Scalable Hierarchical Aggregation and Reduction Protocol (SHARP) Streaming-Aggregation Hardware Design and Evaluation.” International Conference on High Performance Computing, 41–59. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-50743-5_3.

Figure 9 contrasts upstream dataflow in a centralized endpoint reduction against in-switch aggregation. In the endpoint tree of panel (a), intermediate switches merely forward raw packets, forcing all four full gradient tensors (\(\LocalGradient_0\) through \(\LocalGradient_3\)) across the upper links into a single Reducer GPU that executes every addition. In panel (b), SHARP offloads arithmetic to aggregation-capable switch application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs): each intermediate switch computes partial sums (\(\LocalGradient_0+\LocalGradient_1\) and \(\LocalGradient_2+\LocalGradient_3\)) in-flight, leaving only the combined shards for the root switch to finalize. Moving reduction into the network fabric halves the upper link traffic and relieves accelerator endpoints from coordinating arithmetic steps.

SHARP changes both terms of the cost model. On the startup side, it shortens the dependency chain. In a software-based tree reduction, each level waits for an intermediate GPU to receive, reduce, and forward its inputs; with SHARP the switch performs that step in the packet path, so no GPU execution stage or extra endpoint round trip sits between levels. On the byte side, each upper-tier link carries one partial sum rather than every contribution beneath it (figure 9), and in the idealized aggregation tree each GPU sends its contribution up once and receives the result once, where a ring rank sends nearly \(2\CollectivePayloadSize\). Endpoint memory traffic falls for the same reason. Switch processing and contention for aggregation resources add costs of their own, so moving arithmetic into the network changes the \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) terms rather than eliminating them.

Which benefit matters follows the regimes of section 6.4. For small and medium messages, where startup rounds dominate, the shorter dependency chain is the main gain, although for very small messages fixed switch-processing overhead can limit it. For large messages the startup saving becomes proportionally small, and what remains is the byte saving at the endpoints and on the upper tier, which shortens the collective only when those links are the binding resource. Graham et al. (2020) report multi-fold reduction-bandwidth improvements and smaller application-level gains for PyTorch workloads when SHARP Streaming-Aggregation is enabled on HDR/Quantum InfiniBand switches.

Strong scaling campaigns (training a fixed model across more accelerators without proportionally growing batch size) expose the importance of this distinction between collective speed and step time. As the per-accelerator batch shrinks, each gradient tensor stays large (proportional to model size) but the backward pass per accelerator shortens, exposing more of the communication budget. The limiting term can still be bandwidth; strong scaling alone does not establish that startup latency dominates. In-switch aggregation helps when its reduction in endpoint work or fabric traffic shortens the exposed critical path.

SHARP does impose constraints. The switch must support the specific reduction operation and the datatype the job reduces, such as FP16 or BF16 gradients, and supported datatypes differ across switch generations. The number of concurrent aggregation trees is limited by switch resources. Large clusters with many simultaneous training jobs may therefore exhaust aggregation capacity. The HDR/Quantum implementation examined here uses InfiniBand; its measured gains cannot be transferred to an Ethernet deployment without an equivalent, supported aggregation path.

6.7.4 Topology-aware routing

Hierarchy and in-network aggregation change where bytes move and where sums are computed. Their benefit still depends on mapping the logical communication pattern onto physical links. A Ring AllReduce among 64 GPUs creates a logical ring, but the paths carrying its hops can either distribute traffic or concentrate it on shared links. Two runs of the same training job on the same hardware can therefore differ substantially in communication throughput because of rank placement and routing.

Communication libraries like NCCL perform topology detection at initialization and use graph search to choose paths through the hardware topology available to them (NVIDIA 2026). For each pair of ranks, the library must determine whether the peer sits behind a local NVLink switch or 100 meters away across an InfiniBand fabric, because that mapping decides which physical links each logical hop occupies. Local GPU, NVLink, PCIe, and NIC connectivity informs those choices, but local discovery does not reveal every remote switch, route, or competing flow. Fabric configuration and placement information must complement the library’s view when diagnosing cross-node contention.

The topology detection process begins with hardware enumeration. NCCL queries the PCIe bus to discover GPU placement, NVLink connectivity between GPUs, and NIC-to-PCIe-switch affinity. From this information, it constructs an internal graph where nodes represent GPUs and edges represent physical links with annotated bandwidth and latency. A graph search algorithm then finds communication paths that maximize aggregate bandwidth while minimizing the number of cross-domain hops (transitions between NVLink, PCIe, and InfiniBand domains). This topology-aware path selection is what allows NCCL to approach theoretical peak bandwidth on well-configured systems, while topology-unaware implementations can leave a large fraction of bandwidth unused.

Rail-optimized routing (NVIDIA DGX)

In the eight-GPU DGX layout considered here, each GPU has a dedicated inter-node NIC, and the fabric connects matching NIC positions to rail switches. Rail-optimized routing fits the corresponding-shard phase of hierarchical AllReduce: GPU 0 on Node A exchanges its shard with GPU 0 on Nodes B and C, GPU 1 exchanges with peer GPU 1s, and so on. The eight rails can carry these groups concurrently (table 9), subject to their physical capacity and competing traffic. Other collectives may require cross-rail paths.

↩︎ Rail-optimized cabling, which connects each GPU slot position to its own rail switch, is established in Rail-optimized topology.

If all eight shard streams converge on one remote NIC, that endpoint faces 8\(\times\) the balanced receive demand. In the equal-shard layout, matching each shard group to its intended rail distributes one-eighth of the payload to each NIC. This removes that particular concentration; achieving the predicted rate still requires sufficient switch, uplink, and bisection capacity for concurrent traffic.

Rail-optimized routing and hierarchical AllReduce reinforce each other. In the hierarchical decomposition described in section 6.7.1, Step 2 (inter-node AllReduce) naturally aligns with rail topology. GPU \(\LocalRankIndex\) on each node communicates only with GPU \(\LocalRankIndex\) on other nodes, which is precisely the rail pattern. This alignment is not coincidental; NVIDIA designed the DGX hardware with rail-optimized communication in mind, and NCCL’s hierarchical algorithms can exploit this structure when the topology is detected and ranks are mapped correctly. When the logical communication pattern matches the physical topology, each NIC carries its intended share of traffic and the cluster can approach its theoretical peak bisection bandwidth.

Misalignment between logical and physical topology is a common source of performance degradation that is difficult to diagnose without careful profiling. If process ranks are assigned arbitrarily (for example, by the job scheduler without topology awareness), the hierarchical AllReduce may route cross-node traffic through the wrong NICs or across rails, creating hotspots. In the worst case, all eight shard exchanges of a node converge on one NIC, cutting that node’s usable injection bandwidth to one-eighth of the rail-aligned value. Large deployments use topology-aware rank assignment (configured through device ordering such as CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES, NIC binding, and the scheduler’s GPU placement policies) to ensure alignment.

Hierarchical execution and topology-aware routing connect the cost model to a physical placement. The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) terms estimate the service required by each scheduled exchange; hierarchy redistributes work across fabric tiers, and rail alignment can balance corresponding-shard traffic across independent ports. None removes shared-capacity limits. Measured collective duration must confirm that the intended placement shortens the dependency path before the design is accepted.

Self-Check: Question

  1. On an 8-node cluster with 8 GPUs per node, fast local NVLink, and eight independent 50 GB/s network ports per node, how can hierarchical AllReduce improve on a flat ring whose inter-node traffic is serialized through one port?

    1. Hierarchical AllReduce converts the floating-point values to 8-bit integers during the intra-node phase to save inter-node bandwidth.
    2. Flat Ring AllReduce requires every GPU to establish an optical fiber connection directly to all 63 other GPUs in the cluster.
    3. Local ReduceScatter creates one 125 MB shard per GPU from a 1 GB tensor; corresponding-rank AllReduces can distribute these shards across the eight ports, followed by local AllGather.
    4. Hierarchical AllReduce executes without any inter-node network synchronization barriers.

  2. How does dimension-ordered reduction on a 3D torus network (such as Google TPU pods) optimize communication latency and link utilization compared to running a single global logical ring?

    1. It skips communication along the Z-axis by projecting all activations onto a 2D plane.
    2. It executes axis-aligned rings in sequence, reducing startup rounds to \(2[(X-1)+(Y-1)+(Z-1)]\) while trading extra full-message transfers for shorter rings.
    3. It broadcasts full gradient tensors directly to all pod nodes simultaneously over optical circuit switches.
    4. It routes all collective packets through a single central root TPU in the pod to minimize total wire length.

  3. Describe the architectural principle of rail-optimized routing in multi-GPU DGX clusters, and explain the performance pathology that occurs if a job scheduler assigns process ranks without topology awareness.

  4. True or False: On a supported aggregation path, SHARP can move intermediate reduction arithmetic from endpoints into switches, but the resulting latency and training-step benefit still depend on message size, placement, switch resources, and exposed dependencies.

  5. Sequence the three phases of Hierarchical AllReduce executed across a multi-node cluster with \(G\) GPUs per node: (1) intra-node AllGather across local GPUs over NVLink to reconstruct the full globally reduced tensor, (2) inter-node AllReduce across corresponding GPU positions over InfiniBand on the \(M/G\) node shards, (3) intra-node ReduceScatter across local GPUs over NVLink leaving each GPU holding \(1/G\) of the partially reduced tensor.

See Answers →

6.8 AllToAll Exchanges at Scale

Hierarchy, in-network reduction, and rail alignment were all developed for AllReduce, whose uniform payload lets every rank follow the same schedule. The AllToAll rows of table 1, expert parallelism and sharded embedding tables, break that uniformity. As perspective 6.1 showed, an AllToAll finishes when its slowest term finishes: startup across many fine-grained peer messages, injection or reception at an endpoint, traffic across the fabric bisection, or a hotspot created by skew. Production routing makes every one of those terms data-dependent, and the hierarchy just developed for AllReduce reappears as one of the schedules that can contain them.

6.8.1 Dynamic routing and expert imbalance in MoE AllToAll

The worked trace in notebook 6.3 assumes uniform token routing, in which every expert receives an identical share of tokens. In production MoE models, routing decisions are dynamic and data-dependent. The exchange keeps its two-phase shape, but its buffer sizes, its slowest receiver, and its load on the fabric all become functions of the routing distribution.

Each MoE layer performs two exchanges. In an MoE transformer layer with \(\ExpertCount\) experts distributed across \(\DeviceCount\) ranks (\(\ExpertCount/\DeviceCount\) experts per rank), each rank holds a local batch of \(\LocalTokenCount\) tokens. A gating router computes top-\(\RoutedExpertCount\) routing probabilities over all \(\ExpertCount\) experts for each token, inducing two collective communication phases separated by expert computation. During token dispatch, each rank partitions its \(\LocalTokenCount\) local token activation vectors (\(\LayerInputVector\in \mathbb{R}^\HiddenDimension\), where \(\HiddenDimension\) is the model’s hidden dimension) according to the destination ranks hosting the selected experts, executing an AllToAll collective (or variable-size AllToAllv) to route tokens across the fabric according to the routing matrix. After each rank computes forward passes through its assigned local expert feed-forward networks (FFNs), the token combine phase executes a second AllToAll collective to route output vectors back to their originating ranks. Each rank then computes the weighted combination of expert outputs: \[\LayerOutputVector_i = \sum_{j \in \text{top-}\RoutedExpertCount} {\RouterGateProbability}_{i,j} \cdot \text{Expert}_j(\LayerInputVector_i)\]

Because routing is data-dependent, so are the required buffer sizes, even when the input tensor shape is fixed. One simplified model treats assignments of different tokens independently: the count for expert \(\ExpertIndex\) is then \(\ExpertTokenCount\sim \text{Binomial}(\DeviceCount \LocalTokenCount, \ExpertSelectionProbability)\), where \(\ExpertSelectionProbability\) is the fixed probability that a token selects expert \(\ExpertIndex\). Correlated tokens or changing router probabilities require a different count model. To avoid reallocating buffers for every routing decision, a capacity-limited implementation can reserve space using the expert capacity factor \(\ExpertCapacityFactor\ge 1.0\) introduced in Distributed Training: \[\text{Capacity}_\ExpertIndex= \left\lceil \frac{\DeviceCount\times \LocalTokenCount\times \RoutedExpertCount}{\ExpertCount} \times \ExpertCapacityFactor\right\rceil\]

The capacity factor exposes a trade-off between reserved space and retained expert assignments. At uniform expected capacity (\(\ExpertCapacityFactor= 1.0\)), some experts can still overflow while others have unused slots. A dropping implementation bypasses excess assignments through the residual connection, changing the computation and potentially harming training quality. Increasing headroom (\(\ExpertCapacityFactor> 1.0\), for example \(1.25\)–\(1.5\)) can reduce overflow. If the implementation transmits every reserved slot, its padded payload grows by approximately a factor of \(\ExpertCapacityFactor\) relative to the uniform capacity-one budget; kernels that skip unused slots need not perform the corresponding dummy computation. A dropless implementation retains every assignment and can use variable-size AllToAllv to avoid sending unused slots. It still needs enough memory for the realized counts and remains exposed to routing skew.

Skew turns the most heavily loaded rank into a straggler. Because token routing is determined by input semantics, some experts inevitably receive disproportionate token volumes. An expert that receives several times the average load raises its host GPU’s receive traffic by the same excess. The host’s total receive volume depends on the combined load of all experts assigned to it, which is the rank-level quantity in the bound that follows.

Routing skew can create stragglers in both communication and expert computation. An inbound reception bottleneck bounds dispatch completion by the most heavily loaded receiver. Other ranks may finish their transfers earlier, but work that needs the delayed expert outputs must wait. The receive budget gives a lower bound before expert computation and the return exchange are counted: \[\TokenDispatchTime\ge \frac{\MaxReceivedTokenCount\times \HiddenDimension\times \ElementStorageSize}{\ReceiveBandwidth}\] where \(\MaxReceivedTokenCount= \max_\RankIndex \sum_{\ExpertIndex\in \text{rank } \RankIndex} \ExpertTokenCount\) counts the largest remotely received token batch at any rank \(\RankIndex\), restricting \(\ExpertTokenCount\) in this sum to assignments arriving from other ranks; \(\ElementStorageSize\) is the bytes per activation element, and \(\ReceiveBandwidth\) is that rank’s receive bandwidth. Bisection capacity provides a separate limit. Ring AllReduce exchanges data with logical ring neighbors, whereas AllToAll permits a complete bipartite exchange. Under uniform destination assignment, a fraction \((\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\approx 1\) of tokens leaves its source rank; the fraction crossing nodes or a particular fabric cut depends on placement. In the simplified symmetric fabric model with oversubscription ratio \(\OversubscriptionRatio:1\), usable bisection bandwidth \(\BisectionBandwidth = (\DeviceCount\cdot \InjectionBandwidth) / (2\OversubscriptionRatio)\) bounds the time needed to carry traffic across that cut: \[\TokenDispatchTime\ge \frac{\CutCrossingDataVolume}{\BisectionBandwidth}\]

Napkin Math 6.7: The cost of a hot expert rank
Problem: Return to the uniform routing of notebook 6.3: 8 GPUs, 64 experts, 4.096 KB per token, and one 50 GB/s port per GPU. Suppose skew sends one rank’s experts 1.5 times the average remote load. This is an assumed realized routing skew; the per-expert capacity factor reserves total-assignment headroom and does not determine remote arrivals. How does the receive bound on dispatch change?

Math:

  1. Uniform receiver: Each GPU receives 448 remote tokens, or 1.84 MB, so \(\TokenDispatchTime\ge\) 1.84 MB / 50 GB/s \(\approx\) 36.7 μs.
  2. Hot receiver: \(\MaxReceivedTokenCount\) rises to 672 remote tokens, or 2.75 MB, so \(\TokenDispatchTime\ge\) 2.75 MB / 50 GB/s \(\approx\) 55.1 μs.

Systems insight: The exchange cannot finish before its hottest receiver, so a 1.5× load on one rank raises the dispatch bound for the whole layer by the same factor. Transmitting unused capacity slots can add padding bytes; the amount depends on the packing layout rather than on this receive-skew factor alone. A variable-size exchange removes unused-slot traffic but not the receive bound. Expert computation processes local as well as remote assignments, so its work grows by the same factor only if the hot rank’s total local-plus-remote assignment count also grows by that factor, with unchanged work per assignment.

Scale compounds both problems. To amortize small-message overhead across thousands of GPUs, hierarchical MoE runtimes decompose the flat AllToAll into a two-level collective (Hwang et al. 2023). Intra-node NVLink first coalesces tokens destined for experts on the same remote server node. Participating nodes then exchange aggregated payloads over InfiniBand or RoCE, and receiving GPUs use NVLink to distribute tokens to the GPUs hosting the targeted experts. At the logical level, this replaces \(\DeviceCount(\DeviceCount-1)\) directed rank pairs with \(\ClusterNodeCount(\ClusterNodeCount-1)\) directed node pairs; actual transport connections depend on the implementation. Coalescing can improve link utilization and reduce startup overhead, but the token bytes still have to cross the required fabric cuts, and skew can still overload a destination.

6.8.2 AllToAll schedule selection

AllToAll specifies the destination-specific exchange, not the schedule that carries it. The runtime should choose that schedule from the traffic matrix and physical hierarchy rather than treating every exchange as one flat pattern. Table 10 compares the three common regimes; hierarchical designs such as Tutel’s two-dimensional exchange demonstrate why the best schedule can change with message size, scale, and topology (Hwang et al. 2023).

Hwang, Changho, Wei Cui, Yifan Xiong, Ziyue Yang, Ze Liu, Han Hu, Zilong Wang, et al. 2023. “Tutel: Adaptive Mixture-of-Experts at Scale.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 5.

The selection test follows the four-term lower bound developed in perspective 6.1 and section 6.8.1. If per-peer chunks are coarse and source injection or fabric bisection already dominates, coalescing cannot remove the byte term and may only add copies. If \(\DeviceCount\MessageStartupLatency\) dominates because each remote destination receives a small chunk, hierarchy can replace many inter-node startups with fewer aggregate transfers. If counts are skewed, a variable-size interface prevents padding from inflating \(\CutCrossingDataVolume\), but the schedule must still provision for the maximum receive volume and expert compute load. Consequently, a runtime should benchmark candidate schedules with the observed count distribution, rank placement, and topology; a balanced fixed-size microbenchmark cannot select a production AllToAll schedule.

6.9 Gradient Compression Under Bandwidth Scarcity

Even after topology tuning and algorithm selection, a system may remain bandwidth-bound because the physical fabric cannot move gradient payloads fast enough. Reducing the payload can shorten those transfers without changing the fabric. Gradient compression trades numerical fidelity for fewer transmitted bits, so its benefit must be evaluated against the training quality target.

Bandwidth scarcity can arise when training across data centers over wide-area links or on cloud instances with constrained Ethernet networking. A wide-area link offering 10–100\(\times\) less bandwidth than the reference InfiniBand fabric would sharply increase the cost of exchanging the same gradient payload. Beyond the data center, federated training on phone-class devices, the Archetype C (Federated MobileNet) regime, faces constrained and intermittently available cellular or Wi-Fi uplinks. Compression can reduce each client’s update size, although client updates may summarize several local training steps rather than a single gradient. The techniques examined here offer nominal value-payload reductions of 4–1000\(\times\) at different fidelity levels; metadata and aggregation costs reduce the realized savings. The central tension is how much communication can be removed while preserving progress toward the training quality target.

6.9.1 Precision reduction via quantization

Quantization is the least disruptive compression lever when bandwidth is binding because it keeps every gradient element but reduces the bits used to represent it. Most gradients are computed in FP32 (32-bit floating point) or BF16 (16-bit brain float), so reducing bit-width directly reduces communication volume. The useful question is how much gradient fidelity the optimizer can absorb across the progression from mild to aggressive quantization. The compression ratios discussed in this section follow the compression literature and are quoted against FP32 values. The chapter’s 70B payload is already BF16, so against it every ratio halves; INT8 saves 2\(\times\) rather than 4\(\times\), and 1-bit encoding 16\(\times\) rather than 32\(\times\).

Half-precision representations such as FP16 serve as a standard baseline for accelerator training. By halving the bit-width of FP32, FP16 delivers a \(2\times\) reduction in gradient value bytes, provided its range and precision support the workload’s convergence requirements. Moving to INT8 stores each quantized value in one byte, achieving a \(4\times\) reduction relative to FP32 before scale metadata. One symmetric scheme uses 255 levels from negative 127 through positive 127. For a nonzero gradient vector, it computes a tensor-wide scaling factor \(\QuantizationScale= \max(\lvert \LocalGradient\rvert) / 127\), quantizes via \(\QuantizedGradient= \text{round}(\LocalGradient/\QuantizationScale)\), and has the receiver reconstruct \(\ReconstructedGradient= \QuantizedGradient\times \QuantizationScale\); an all-zero vector requires a separate zero case. Independently scaled integer vectors cannot be added directly to recover the sum of their reconstructed gradients. The collective must account for each scale and use sufficient accumulator precision, or reconstruct values before reduction. At the extreme, 1-bit SGD transmits only the sign of each gradient element (\(+1\) or \(-1\)), yielding a \(32\times\) reduction in value bits over FP32. The receiver reconstructs values using a learned or adaptive scaling factor, though the substantial noise introduced by 1-bit quantization can severely degrade convergence without error-feedback mechanisms (Seide et al. 2014; Karimireddy et al. 2019).

Block quantization for gradient communication

Using a single scaling factor for an entire tensor makes every element’s quantization resolution depend on the largest magnitude. When magnitudes vary substantially across the tensor, small gradients may round to zero even in regions whose values could be represented accurately with a smaller scale. Block quantization addresses this by dividing the gradient tensor into blocks of \(\QuantizationBlockSize\) elements (for example, \(\QuantizationBlockSize= 64\) or \(128\)) and computing an independent scaling factor per block. Each block’s scaling factor adapts to its local range, improving resolution where that range is smaller at the cost of transmitting \(\GradientElementCount/\QuantizationBlockSize\) additional scaling factors for evenly divided blocks.

For a gradient vector of dimension \(\GradientElementCount\) quantized to INT8 with block size \(\QuantizationBlockSize\), the message size is \(\GradientElementCount\times 1\ \text{byte}\) for the quantized values plus \((\GradientElementCount/\QuantizationBlockSize) \times 4\ \text{bytes}\) for the FP32 scaling factors, or \(\GradientElementCount+ 4\GradientElementCount/\QuantizationBlockSize\) bytes in total. The effective compression is therefore \(4\GradientElementCount/ (\GradientElementCount+ 4\GradientElementCount/\QuantizationBlockSize) = 4\QuantizationBlockSize/ (\QuantizationBlockSize+ 4)\), which for \(\QuantizationBlockSize= 128\) reaches 3.88\(\times\), close to the theoretical 4\(\times\). For rounding to the nearest level without clipping, the absolute error bound changes from \(\QuantizationScale\cdot 0.5\), where \(\QuantizationScale\) is the global scaling factor, to \(\BlockQuantizationScale\cdot 0.5\), where \(\BlockQuantizationScale\) is the block-local scaling factor. Blocks with smaller ranges obtain tighter bounds; the improvement depends on how magnitudes are distributed among blocks.

Interfaces that fuse quantization and reduction must preserve the meaning of each block’s scale throughout aggregation. They must also control the resulting numerical error. Deterministic rounding generally introduces bias, whereas suitable randomized quantizers preserve the input in expectation while adding variance. Error feedback addresses accumulated compression error, but the need for it depends on the compressor and optimizer rather than on bit-width alone. Quantization should therefore move only as far down the precision ladder as the convergence budget permits.

6.9.2 Gradient sparsification methods

Quantization attacks communication volume by reducing the number of bits per gradient element while transmitting every element. An orthogonal approach, sparsification, attacks the problem from the other direction: keep full precision but transmit only a subset of gradient elements, setting the rest to zero.

Top-k sparsification

The most common method is Top-k compression: for a gradient vector \(\LocalGradient\in \mathbb{R}^{\GradientElementCount}\), transmit only the \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\) elements with the largest absolute magnitude, setting the rest to zero (Aji and Heafield 2017; Lin et al. 2018):

\[\text{TopK}(\LocalGradient) = \LocalGradient\odot \TopMagnitudeIndicator\]

where \(|\LocalGradient|_{(\RetainedGradientElementCount)}\) is the \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\)-th largest element by magnitude. The threshold expression retains all ties; an implementation targeting an exact count must break ties at the boundary. With \(\RetainedGradientElementCount= 0.001 \times \GradientElementCount\) (keeping only 0.1 percent of elements), the retained values occupy 1000\(\times\) less space before index overhead.

The compression headline must be discounted by the cost of encoding the sparse representation. Transmitting a sparse gradient requires sending both the nonzero values and their indices. For a gradient vector of dimension \(\GradientElementCount\) with \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\) nonzero elements, the encoded message contains \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\) values (each in FP32 or FP16) plus \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\) indices (typically INT32). The total message size is \(\RetainedGradientElementCount\times (4 + 4) = 8 \RetainedGradientElementCount\) bytes for FP32 values with INT32 indices. The effective compression ratio is therefore \(4\GradientElementCount/ (8 \RetainedGradientElementCount) = \GradientElementCount/ (2 \RetainedGradientElementCount)\).

For \(\RetainedGradientElementCount= 0.001 \GradientElementCount\), the encoded message yields a compression ratio of 500\(\times\), not the 1000\(\times\) suggested by the kept-element fraction alone, because the index overhead is significant. Using a 16-bit unsigned index representation (for \(\GradientElementCount< 65536\)) or run-length encoding for suitable sparsity patterns can improve the effective ratio. Aggregation imposes a separate cost. Workers may retain different coordinates, so summing their packed value arrays would combine unrelated parameters. A sparse collective must match indices and sum contributions to each coordinate; the union of selected coordinates can be much larger than any one worker’s selection. Local compression ratios therefore do not directly specify collective traffic or runtime.

Top-k is not the only sparsification rule. Random-k sparsification selects \(\RandomRetainedElementCount\) elements uniformly at random and scales them by \(\GradientElementCount/\RandomRetainedElementCount\) to maintain an unbiased gradient estimate. Random-k has the advantage of being an unbiased compressor even without error feedback, because \(\mathbb{E}[\text{RandomK}(\LocalGradient)] = \LocalGradient\). Its random selection can discard large components, and rescaling the retained values introduces variance. Top-k retains the largest components but introduces bias. Their convergence behavior therefore depends on the optimizer, residual correction, and gradient distribution; unbiasedness alone does not determine which reaches a quality target sooner.

The convergence problem

Naively discarding small gradients with Top-k creates a systematic bias. If a parameter’s gradient remains below the selection threshold, even a consistent contribution of 0.01 per step is repeatedly omitted. Over thousands of steps, those omissions can materially change the optimization trajectory. The problem is persistent exclusion, which can occur even when each discarded component looks negligible in isolation.

The bias of uncorrected Top-k means that the usual argument for SGD with an unbiased gradient estimate no longer applies directly. Biased compression can cause convergence failure even when the uncompressed method converges. Deterministic quantization can also repeatedly omit small contributions; randomized quantization and rescaled Random-k instead require accounting for their added variance. Error feedback addresses accumulated compression error for quantized gradients (Seide et al. 2014) and for sparsified gradients (Stich et al. 2018; Karimireddy et al. 2019).

6.9.3 Error feedback and residual accumulation

Error feedback controls the accumulated error introduced by compression. At step \(\OptimizerStepIndex\), the system adds the local residual \(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_t\) to the new gradient \(\LocalGradient_t\), compresses that corrected vector, and transmits the compressed update \(\CompressedGradientUpdate_t\). The next local accumulator stores the difference between the corrected vector and the transmitted update. This residual path defers information so that later updates can recover it under the compressor’s convergence assumptions.

Error feedback carries the residual into the next compressed step.

Definition 6.3: Error feedback mechanism

Error feedback is a distributed-training technique for gradient compression that maintains a per-worker residual accumulator \(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_t\), re-injecting the compression error back into the next gradient update so that information omitted from the transmitted update remains in the residual. A finite run can end with an untransmitted residual.

  1. Significance: Top-k sparsification at 1 percent keeps only the largest 1 percent of gradient values by magnitude, reducing each worker’s retained value payload from 140 GB to 1.4 GB for a full 70B gradient, a 100\(\times\) reduction before sparse-index and aggregation overhead. Without error feedback, the discarded 99 percent of gradient values create a biased update that can harm convergence. With error feedback, omitted information remains in the residual and can influence later selections, supporting the convergence guarantees analyzed for memory/error-feedback variants under their assumptions (Stich et al. 2018; Karimireddy et al. 2019).
  2. Distinction: Unlike plain lossy compression, which permanently discards sub-threshold gradient components, error feedback delays their transmission. A component too small to send in one step stays in the residual and can influence later transmitted updates as it accumulates; a finite run may still end with an untransmitted residual.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that error feedback is automatic whenever a compressor is used. It is not: the training system must explicitly preserve and reinject the residual. Without that residual path, Top-k and 1-bit quantization introduce systematic bias that can accumulate across steps (Karimireddy et al. 2019).

Error feedback preserves cumulative gradient information over time. Consider what happens over \(\TrainingStepCount\) steps:

\[\sum_{t=1}^{\TrainingStepCount} \CompressedGradientUpdate_t = \sum_{t=1}^{\TrainingStepCount} \left[(\LocalGradient_t + \ErrorFeedbackResidual_t) - \ErrorFeedbackResidual_{t+1}\right] = \sum_{t=1}^{\TrainingStepCount} \LocalGradient_t + \ErrorFeedbackResidual_1 - \ErrorFeedbackResidual_{\TrainingStepCount+1}\]

If the error accumulator remains bounded under the chosen compressor and training assumptions, then as \(\TrainingStepCount\to \infty\):

\[\frac{1}{\TrainingStepCount}\sum_{t=1}^{\TrainingStepCount} \CompressedGradientUpdate_t \to \frac{1}{\TrainingStepCount}\sum_{t=1}^{\TrainingStepCount} \LocalGradient_t\]

Under that bounded-residual condition, the difference between the two averages vanishes. At a finite stopping point, untransmitted information remains in the residual. Repeated small gradients can accumulate in \(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_t\) until they exceed the compression threshold, as the worked trace illustrates.

The telescoping property of compression error across time is why error feedback can transform a biased compressor into a convergent training method. Theoretical analyses show convergence guarantees for sparsified SGD with memory and for EF-SGD with broad compression operators under their stated assumptions (Stich et al. 2018; Karimireddy et al. 2019). A short trace shows how error feedback preserves gradient information that naive compression would lose. Table 11 runs the trace for a rounding compressor with no residual path. It transmits each value rounded to the nearest whole unit, so any value below 0.5 is sent as zero:

Table 12 runs the same sequence with a residual accumulator \(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_t\) that re-injects the discarded remainder into the next step.

Example 6.2: Error feedback mechanism
Scenario: A gradient compression pipeline rounds each gradient value to the nearest whole unit before transmission, so values below 0.5 are sent as zero, over 5 training steps of distributed data-parallel training.

Diagnosis: Naive rounding (table 11) discards every value below 0.5, transmitting 0 when the true cumulative gradient is 1.6 (100 percent gradient loss). With error feedback (table 12), the residual grows until it crosses 0.5 and is sent, so the run transmits 2 and ends holding −0.4 in the residual. Transmitted plus residual, 2 + (−0.4), equals the true total of 1.6.

Systems lesson: The trace illustrates how error feedback carries omitted information into later updates. By storing untransmitted residual vectors in local accelerator memory (\(\ErrorFeedbackResidual_{t+1} = \LocalGradient_t + \ErrorFeedbackResidual_t - \CompressedGradientUpdate_t\)), repeated small contributions can accumulate until they cross the transmission threshold. The trace demonstrates the residual mechanism; convergence still depends on the compressor and optimizer assumptions.

6.9.4 1-bit Adam compression-aware optimization

Error feedback can restore convergence guarantees for suitable compressors under stated assumptions. When applied directly to gradients, it corrects the transmitted gradient without using the optimizer’s internal state. Momentum and second-moment estimates offer other possible quantities to communicate, but compressing them changes how error enters the optimization trajectory. The decision is whether compression should operate on raw gradients or on optimizer state, and what analysis supports that choice.

A more integrated approach, pioneered by Microsoft’s DeepSpeed team, compresses the optimizer’s communication rather than the raw gradients. 1-bit Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) (Tang et al. 2021) exploits the observed stabilization of Adam’s second-moment (variance) estimate after an initial training period. It then compresses the communicated first-moment state, the momentum, to 1 bit per parameter plus a scaling factor while retaining the stabilized second moment as an adaptive preconditioner.

The algorithm proceeds in two phases. During a warmup phase, standard Adam runs with full-precision communication so that the second-moment estimate stabilizes. The algorithm then freezes that variance estimate and uses it as a fixed adaptive preconditioner. In the communication-efficient phase, workers communicate the momentum using 1-bit compression (sign plus scale) and retain each compression residual locally so it can be added before the next compression step.

The theoretical justification combines the frozen adaptive preconditioner with error-compensated momentum compression. The residual feeds quantization error into later momentum messages, controlling accumulated compression error and supporting the paper’s convergence result without treating the compressed update as an unbiased estimator.

The 1-bit Adam paper reports up to 5\(\times\) communication-volume reduction while matching the convergence speed of uncompressed Adam, with experiments up to 256 GPUs showing up to 3.3\(\times\) higher throughput for BERT-Large pretraining and up to 2.9\(\times\) higher throughput for Stanford Question Answering Dataset (SQuAD) fine-tuning (Tang et al. 2021). The practical lesson is narrower than “compression always wins”: the compression overhead must remain small compared with the communication time saved.

The success of 1-bit Adam illustrates how the optimizer’s structure can guide a compression scheme. Freezing the second moment and correcting compressed momentum together support the method’s convergence analysis; replacing every gradient with its sign would not reproduce that algorithm. The reported end-to-end volume reduction of up to 5\(\times\) includes the uncompressed warmup and other communication costs, so it is smaller than the raw 32\(\times\) ratio of 1-bit values to FP32 momentum.

6.9.5 Bandwidth versus convergence trade-offs in compression

Gradient compression is not free; it trades reduced communication for increased variance or bias in the optimization process. The comparison in table 13 summarizes the source-backed patterns from quantization, sparsification, error-feedback, and optimizer-aware compression work (Seide et al. 2014; Aji and Heafield 2017; Lin et al. 2018; Stich et al. 2018; Karimireddy et al. 2019; Tang et al. 2021).

Seide, Frank, Hao Fu, Jasha Droppo, Gang Li, and Dong Yu. 2014. “1-Bit Stochastic Gradient Descent and Its Application to Data-Parallel Distributed Training of Speech DNNs.” Interspeech 2014, 1058–62. https://doi.org/10.21437/interspeech.2014-274.
Aji, Alham Fikri, and Kenneth Heafield. 2017. “Sparse Communication for Distributed Gradient Descent.” Proceedings of the 2017 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing, 440–45. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/d17-1045.
Lin, Yujun, Song Han, Huizi Mao, Yu Wang, and William J Dally. 2018. “Deep Gradient Compression: Reducing the Communication Bandwidth for Distributed Training.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Stich, S. U., J.-B. Cordonnier, and M. Jaggi. 2018. “Sparsified SGD with Memory.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 31.
Karimireddy, Sai Praneeth, Quentin Rebjock, Sebastian U. Stich, and Martin Jaggi. 2019. “Error Feedback Fixes SignSGD and Other Gradient Compression Schemes.” Proceedings of the 36th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 97: 3252–61.
Tang, Hanlin, Shaoduo Gan, Ammar Ahmad Awan, Samyam Rajbhandari, Conglong Li, Xiangru Lian, Ji Liu, Ce Zhang, and Yuxiong He. 2021. “1-Bit Adam: Communication Efficient Large-Scale Training with Adam’s Convergence Speed.” Proceedings of the 38th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 139: 10118–29.

Decision criteria for gradient compression

The decision rule is not the compression ratio alone. Compression is worthwhile only when the wall-clock time it saves across the run exceeds its encoding overhead plus the cost of any extra steps that slower convergence requires, so the comparison must be made in time to the same quality target. A high communication-computation ratio \(\CommunicationComputeRatio= \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)/(\IronLawComputeTime/\DeviceCount)\), the ratio The Physics of the Cluster used to separate compute-bound from communication-bound clusters, creates an opportunity when that communication is exposed on the critical path. The compressor and optimizer analysis must establish how bias or variance is controlled, including whether error feedback is required; convergence tests then check that the chosen configuration reaches the target.

The first test is per synchronization. Compression shrinks only the byte term, so it helps a payload above \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), such as a full gradient or a large bucket, and does little for one below it, whose startup term remains (section 6.4). Even above \(\LatencyBandwidthCrossoverSize\), encoding must cost less than the transfer time it removes.

Napkin Math 6.8: The payback of compression
Problem: A training job takes 40 ms to synchronize a gradient buffer. A compression scheme shrinks the payload, but after scale factors, metadata, and protocol overhead its assumed effective throughput gain is 8×, and encoding and decoding add 2 ms of accelerator time per synchronization. Does compression shorten the synchronization, and by how much?

Math:

  1. Compressed communication time: 40 ms divided by the effective factor 8 = 5 ms.
  2. Total new time: 5 ms (comm) + 2 ms (overhead) = 7 ms.
  3. Effective speedup: 40 ms divided by 7 ms \(\approx\) 5.7×.

Systems insight: Compression is a trade of compute for bandwidth. With compression ratio \(\CompressionRatio\) and encoding cost \(\CodecOverheadTime\), it pays off only when \(\CodecOverheadTime< \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount) \times (1 - 1/\CompressionRatio)\), that is, when encoding costs less than the communication time it removes. In this case, spending 2 ms to save 35 ms of network time is a clear win. On a high-speed NVLink network where \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) is only 1 ms, the same compression logic would cost more than it saves and slow down training. Always profile the network before adding compression.

The second test spans the run, because a compressor that passes the per-step test can still lose once slower convergence adds steps.

Suppose a training run requires 100,000 steps to reach its quality target without compression, with each step taking 500 ms (300 ms compute and 200 ms communication), for a total of 50,000 s. INT8 compression with error feedback of an FP32 payload reduces communication by 4× (from 200 ms to 50 ms per step), but suppose it raises the required steps by 10 percent, to 110,000. The new total is 110,000 steps at 350 ms each, or 38,500 s, a 23 percent improvement. Compression wins here because the per-step saving of 150 ms outweighs the additional steps.

The same model on a faster network, where communication takes only 30 ms per step, reverses the verdict. Compression cuts that to 7.5 ms, saving 22.5 ms per step, but still adds 10,000 steps at 307.5 ms each. The run now takes 33,825 s instead of 33,000 s, 2.5 percent longer.
